Preface 

THE COMPLEXITIES of modern business life have placed an increased emphasis both on record planning and record keepingin short, on the function of accounting. As the arbiter to and the servant of business the banker must be alert to utilize and interpret the information made available to him by the accountant. 

The banker in serving his community becomes aware of the need for accounting records, and he should be equipped to suggest the kinds and types of records ordinarily maintained both for business and for personal needs.

Instalment credit men are in agreement that personal records are essential to a householder when borrowing to purchase on time such durable consumer goods as a home, an automobile, or any one or more of a number of household appliances. Personal records are also useful in preparing or in authenticating tax returns.

Accounting is one of the three great fields of learning that underlie the banking business. Law and economics are its companion fields; in both of these fields, it is necessary to rely on the detailed information produced by accounting records. A knowledge of accounting is fundamental to any career in banking, whether it be in investments, credits, trusts, operations, or even in personnel (value per man-hour is often computed). Senior executive and administrative positions cannot be attained without a knowledge of accounting and an understanding of the accounting function, particularly in accounting for control. 

The responsible executive managers of a bank must have a knowledge of accounting not only as it relates to the businesses of their customers but also as it relates to the activities of the bank itself. Thoroughly understood and accurately interpreted, accounting records constitute one of the most effective tools available to management in making intelligent decisions. In a bank, any planned program for the development of management personnel must include training in accounting and briefing in the modern techniques for collecting data. 5-37-62 1001332

The study of accounting is an integral part of the curriculum of the American Institute of Banking. The course in accounting is a prerequisite to the courses in Analyzing Financial Statements and in Credit Administration. It provides the essential background for the study of trusts, investments, and such courses in credits as Instalment Credit, Agricultural Credit, and Home Mort gage Lending.

The study of accounting may be viewed as a series of experiments in which all the factors and forces of business are brought together under controlled conditions. This approach is intended to increase the student's appreciation and understanding of fundamental relationships rather than concentrate his attention on rules and routines to be memorized. Thus each new advance, each new problem is presented as a live business situation confronting the business manager. In studying the basic concepts of accounting and in working a well organized series of problems, the student in effect lives within the business situation and experiences the ebb and flow of basic business forces. It is also necessary to study data-collecting techniques in order to develop both an appreciation of the capacities as well as a critical concept of the weaknesses in the data-collecting process. Armed with this training, the banker knows what the business manager should expect of his accountant and what safeguards should be used to avoid placing too great a faith in figures, a faith not warranted in view of the risks inherent in gathering data.

The Institute is deeply grateful to the author of this volume, Leo A. Schmidt, professor of accounting, School of Business Ad ministration, University of Michigan, Ann Arbor, Michigan. The author is a prominent educator who has written widely on the subject of accounting. His work for the Institute of Accountants, in the field of standardized examination materials, is especially well known. Professor Schmidt has also written several definitive texts on accounting.

Serving on the critic committee with him were Jesse H. Lawrence, assistant treasurer, The Chase Manhattan Bank, New York, New York; Raymond L. Mannix, M.B.A., C.P.A., professor of accounting, chairman of the department, College of Business 

Administration, Boston University, Boston, Massachusetts; and 

Oscar K. Thornton, C.P.A. and practicing commercial accountant, Dallas, Texas. Each of these three members of the critic committee is an experienced instructor of Institute classes in New York, Boston, and Dallas chapters. To each of these men the Instivi

tute owes a deep debt of gratitude for his patience and thoroughness in rebuilding the Institute text in accounting. 

It is hoped that the student will gain an insight into the basic functions and uses of accountancy from the management viewpoint and that this knowledge will give him a proper perspective on the relationship which accounting bears to banking and to business.

LEROY LEWIS National Educational Director vii

ACCOUNTING I

ACCOUNTING I 

Part 1 

MANAGEMENT AND BUSINESS INFORMATION CHAPTER I  

THE SYSTEMATIC approach to any technical subject requires a clear definition of the subject matter and an understanding of the purpose behind the proposed study. This task does not sound like a difficult one, for obviously the subject matter of this course is business accounting and the motive for studying it is to learn about management. A really careful answer to the questions, "Just what is business?" and "Just what do we mean by management of business?" will be found to be the best possible starting point for the study of accounting.

Every beginning class in accounting may well spend an hour or two in trying to push ahead on its own in this inquiry: What is really meant by management? What does a manager actually do? Name fifteen or twenty acts that are clearly management which a grocery store manager might do during a typical day. 

Are unlocking the door, turning up the thermostat, and opening the safe acts of management? What differentiates mere work or labor from management in any business establishment? Is management primarily a matter of telling other people what to do? Is management primarily performing the difficult tasks, while labor is performing the easy tasks? Is management a matter of doing the brain work while labor is doing the physical work? Are management acts always clearly distinguishable from labor chores? Is the manager concerned only with management or does he often labor 1

also? Is the manager always clearly aware of which parts of his activity are management and which are labor? Does the relation between the labor and the management elements of a manager's activity change as we go from very small to larger and larger businesses? What is the relationship between ownership and management?

After considering the above questions carefully, try to identify and describe the essential element in management. 

Practically speaking, the question "what is meant by management?" cannot be answered until an answer is found to the question, "what is business?" It may be useful at this time to clarify the more basic problem. Many human activities which exist primarily for social, recreational, charitable, and governmental purposes have certain "business" aspects, and the conduct of these activities presents many typical problems of business management. 

For the purposes of this study of accounting, however, we will confine our attention to privately-owned business units which exist for the primary purpose of earning a profit for the owners. 

This definition will provide a practical background against which we can do our thinking about business. Incidentally, the concepts developed against the strictly business background will be applicable, by extension, to many situations in which the business aspects of an enterprise are secondary to other major purposes. 

In the broad sense accounting is simply the study of the fundamental nature of business. In the narrowest sense, accounting is the process of recording business activity, that is, of getting the facts of business down in black on white. This record must be the clearest possible portrayal of the essential facts, happenings, and relationships of business. Furthermore, to be really useful, it must not be a mere chronology of every event that happened. It must be a marshaling of those events, a marshaling which sorts out the essential facts from the nonessential, which brings like items together, which recognizes significant relationships between the various categories of items, and which exhibits all the facts in a summarized, understandable, useful form. 

It should now be obvious that the narrower concept of accounting, that is, the making of an adequate record, is inextricably related to the broader concept, that is, the critical examination of the real nature of business and the problems of management. An

accountant who does not understand the problems of business as such would certainly provide an unsatisfactory record of business facts. All the techniques and routines of record making should be planned entirely in the light of their bearing upon the practical problems of management.

In the foregoing paragraphs two very important and closely related principles have been introduced, namely, the real nature of business management and the relation of accounting to business management. These principles must be applied to some simple real-life situations, both to consolidate our understanding of the vital subject matter of this chapter and to demonstrate the way in which principles should always be tried out or tested by application to practical problems. 

A Practical Application of the Management Concept 

During the course of one day, Tom Jones, the owner of a grocery store, did the following things. Which of them do you regard as management and which as labor? What is the essential element that distinguishes management actions? What kinds of information could have been collected that would have helped Mr. Jones make each of his management decisions? Try to answer these questions for yourself and then check your answers against the interpretations in the text following the problem. 

1. At 7 a.m. Mr. Jones unlocked the door of the store, turned on the air conditioning, and swept the sidewalk. 

2. He telephoned the wholesale house and ordered a new line of canned goods to replace the line which he had carried previously. 

3. He dismissed an employee who was late for the fifth time in the week. 

4. He reconciled his bank balance. 

5. He redecorated the show window. 

6. He decided to buy his baked goods from the Wilson Bakery rather than from his previous supplier, Smith & Company. 

7. He interviewed three applicants and hired one as an additional clerk. 

8. He took inventory of his stock of canned goods in the basement. 

9. He decided to keep the store open on Friday evenings.

10. He decided to raise his prices on coffee and spent three quarters of an hour re-marking the cans and bags on the shelves. 

11. He signed checks for the weekly payroll. 

12. He arranged to have his $5,000 note extended by the bank, since paying it would reduce his balance to an uncomfortably small amount.

Let us discuss each item of this problem carefully in order to understand the principles involved. 

1. Unlocking the door, turning on the air conditioning, and sweeping the sidewalk are all simple acts of labor. 

2. The decision to take on the new line of canned goods is a management activity. The actual making of the telephone call is labor. Mr. Jones had to decide between the following alternatives: continuing with the old line, ordering the particular new line, or possibly ordering some other line. Useful information in helping him make this decision would be the trend of sales (upward or downward) of the old line, the gross margin or profit (that is, selling price minus cost price) on the old line compared to the margin obtainable on the new line, and the record of returned sales on the old line which would indicate customer dissatisfaction. 

3. The decision to dismiss an employee is often a difficult one. 

The alternative is to keep him and risk the spread of his habit of tardiness to other employees. Presumably Mr. Jones talked with the employee and ascertained that there were no circumstances which provided an acceptable excuse for his repeated offense. In so small a business the manager may rely on his own observation of the employees' attendance, but in a larger business specific records would be kept, possibly with the aid of time clocks, to prevent uncertainty regarding an employee's performance record. 

Other records concerning the employee's service, such as the record of his raises in pay and the record of his sales, would also be important to Mr. Jones. Jones might decide to permit considerable tardiness if this particular employee is his star salesman. In this case Jones has presumably verified all the evidence and decided to take the unpleasant step of dismissing the employee. 

4. In reconciling his bank balance Jones must exercise knowledge, skill, and care, but he does not have to make a serious choice between alternatives.

5. The actual decoration of a window is a special kind of skilled labor. Behind this task may lie such management problems as what kinds of goods to feature in the window, how often to change the display, whether to use a deep-window display or to build the exhibits close to the glass. Obviously, in this case it is difficult to draw the line between management and labor. 

Whether to pile the cans in the exhibit three tiers high or four tiers high is a decision that probably does not involve consequences serious enough to be considered management. 

6. The decision to change his supplier of baked goods is a decision between alternatives. Records of sales trends, prices, returns, and the like would be similar to those mentioned in item 2, and they would be useful, if available. In many businesses such information might not be immediately available, but it might be found in the underlying records. In weighing all this information and in making the final decision, Jones is clearly performing a management function. 

7. The choice of the right employees is an extremely important decision since it affects many other events. This decision does not involve the records of the business. The manager might give the applicant certain tests, he might ask for references, and he might require a probationary period during which he could watch the new employee's work, but the decision to hire must be based largely on his subjective evaluation of the new man. 

8. In taking the inventory, Jones must make some very compli cated decisions such as what basis is to be used in pricing the inventory, how frequently the inventory count must be taken for proper control of the stock, what principle is to be followed in pricing goods which are or may become obsolete. These related decisions certainly involve management activities, but the actual routine work of taking the inventory is properly classified as labor. 

9. The decision to keep the store open on Friday evenings is a serious decision, involving public relations, employee relations, and competition, which requires very careful consideration. Figure information probably could have an important bearing on Jones's decision if he studied a careful presentation of sales trends by daily periods. For instance, if Jones's Friday and Saturday sales have dropped fifteen per cent since his competitor began keeping open Friday nights a month ago, Jones had better know that fact.

Another important fact that Jones should consider is the number of hours per week which his clerks are working. If the additional hours of work on Friday evening would necessitate the payment of wages at the time and a half rate, Mr. Jones had better know that fact. In this problem, information from sources entirely outside the business may have very great significance. If the reports of the grocery trade association in Jones's area show that more and more stores are keeping open on Friday evenings with satisfactory results, this information may be the determining factor in Jones's decision. Information from an outside source certainly is very important to management, and the good accountant ought to realize its significance and to help management obtain such information when it is available. An acute awareness of all possible sources of information bearing on business decisions is the mark of the skilful manager. 

10. In a retail trading business, any decision to change prices is important and delicate. If the cost has increased, the selling price must also increase, unless Jones wishes to make a special price appeal to his customers by taking less than his normal margin of profit. If Jones raises his prices too much or too soon, his customers may react very unfavorably. During a period when prices are changing rapidly on a critical item like coffee, Jones should study his coffee sales separately from his other sales in order to watch the effect of his pricing policy more closely. 

11. The signing of payroll checks may be quite a routine task, and thus it may be merely labor. If, however, Jones uses this task as the occasion to review the adequacy of the amounts paid to employees, such thinking may properly be considered management. 

If the clerks' pay includes commissions on the sale of certain types of goods or bonuses for making a sales "quota," the apparently simple act of signing the pay checks may involve detailed record keeping, however, even such intricate detail is merely labor. 

12. Jones's decision to have his note extended is one requiring financial judgment. This type of decision is often considered to be at the very apex of management activity. Such a decision is influenced by budgeting and forecasting considerations rather than by the record of past and present facts. To decide rightly whether to renew his loan, Mr. Jones must consider his present cash balance, together with all sources of cash receipts and all

probable expenditures in the next few months. The rate at which cash will be derived from receivables and inventory, the due dates of accounts payable and of other liabilities presently on his books, as well as all other foreseeable expenditures such as wages, utilities, and the like, must be taken into account. Mr. Jones may try to estimate these figures mentally or he may set up a formal cash budget, but he must make a budget if he wishes to reach an intelligent decision.

It should be quite clear that the essence of management is the making of serious decisions between alternative courses of action. 

Certain acts of management may be closely related to labor, especially when the labor is mental labor. Very minor decisions, which are perhaps technically acts of management, may be left in the hands of junior employees. The act that characterizes management is one in which the choice to be made has an important bearing on the fortunes of the business. 

The clerk in the produce department makes decisions when he sorts the vegetables, separating the spoiled vegetables from the good ones, but this act can hardly be called management. In contrast, a decision to discontinue the produce department entirely or to expand it to twice its present capacity would certainly be a significant management decision. 

MANAGEMENT IN LARGER BUSINESSES 

The problems illustrated previously were drawn from the situations arising in a very small business in which all acts of management were concentrated in one person, Mr. Jones, the owner. 

As a business grows larger, however, the owners usually delegate to employees substantial parts of the management function. In very large businesses this delegation of authority may result in the virtual separation of ownership and management with the real ownership of the business resting in the hands of thousands of stockholders, many of whom do not even attend the once-a-year stockholders' meeting.

Thus practically all management authority is delegated by the stockholders to a small group at the top-management level, and a further delegation of authority is made by top management down through the successive echelons of management.

The basic characteristic of management, however, is still the making of a choice between possible alternative courses of action. 

Almost always the manager should have a large amount of factual information to help him in making his choice. This information is necessary whether the decision is being made by an owner-manager or by a manager exercising a delegated duty. 

Large Business and the Principle of Control 

The delegation of management authority in a large business creates an entirely new management problem. Each delegation of authority from a higher echelon to a lower one creates the problem of measuring the effectiveness with which the delegated authority is handled. It is commonly pointed out that authority and responsibility must go hand in hand. Therefore, if the delegation of the authority is to be effective, some provision must be made for the measurement of the responsibility. 

Each person in the organization must have the means of knowing whether the authority which he has delegated to those below him is being efficiently and economically exercised. He can rely to some small extent upon his own direct observation, but to a greater and greater extent in a large business he must rely on formal reports that are made to him through the accounting system for the basic purpose of measuring the performance of the units below him in the business structure. 

The annual income statement and statement of condition are the means of reporting to the stockholders how well top management has run the business. These statements, especially the income statement, are presented in greater detail in reporting to top management on the success of the various major operating divisions of the business, each of which is headed by a superintendent or general manager. These officials in turn require more detailed statements showing the costs and the performance of each department under their control; reports are required at each supervisory level down to the lowest level at which we can distinguish any delegation of management authority. This practice of measuring the performance of responsible persons throughout the organization is known as accounting for control. In terms of the actual welfare of a business, accounting for control is probably the most important feature of modern accounting. It is the real-

istic implementation of the principle that responsibility and authority must go hand in hand.

The importance of the principle can be brought out effectively by asking what the situation would be in a large business with a complicated scheme of management delegations if accounting for control were entirely absent. Under such conditions many of the departments might be extremely inefficient in their operations year after year without detection. Since human beings are naturally lazy, the inefficiency in a business would tend to increase and would spread to other departments if there were no control over a situation such as we have described. In a small business the owner-manager would see what is occurring without formal records and would take steps to correct any adverse condition. In the large business, however, the owners and even the top managers are so far away from the actual situation that they would never know in which area the trouble is occurring. 

Eventually, of course, such spreading inefficiency would be reflected in greatly reduced net profits or in net losses, but even then the owners and general managers would be helpless to correct the situation if the accounts did not give effective control. Top management could start campaigns with much oratory about the need for greater efficiency, but each department head could disclaim the responsibility for inefficiency and accuse other departments. 

In the absence of factual accounting proof, it is quite easy for the typical department head to believe that his department is run economically and efficiently.

Contrast this situation with the one in which the accounting system reflects clearly, month after month, what is happening to costs in each department and measures each department's performance accurately. As a matter of fact, this situation develops in most large businesses in which competent accountants have fully established the principle of accounting for control. 

The Danger of the Medium Sized Business 

Many medium sized and smaller businesses have not yet arrived at a mature development of this aspect of accounting, that is, accounting for control. When they have grown beyond the stage of complete personal supervision and have not yet achieved control through accounting, these businesses are in a very dangerous

position. At this point the banker, acting as a consultant to business, can probably make his greatest contribution to business development by understanding the principle of accounting for control and by explaining and suggesting its use in the businesses of his clients.

In the normal growth of a business, there may be a period of transition lasting several years when its management is passing from the personal control of a single owner-manager to the maturity of a functional organization with adequate accounting control. Some businesses go through this period with comparative ease, some struggle through it after years of hardship, and some fail in the struggle. Some customers are problems to a bank only because they are having difficulties in making this transition. 

Some of these difficulties cannot be avoided, others can be greatly ameliorated if they are understood. 

SUMMARY 

In this chapter a preview of the real nature of business management has been presented. Basically, management is the making of decisions with respect to the alternative courses of action which the business may take in its relations with the business world. A closely related problem of every large business is that of policing the performance of its own units to guarantee their efficient operation.

In both aspects management leans heavily on the availability of pertinent and reliable information. The supplying of this information is the function of accounting. Our study of accounting will be soundly grounded if we keep this function constantly in mind. 

A useful and realistic approach to practical accounting is an inquiry into what kinds of information the management of any business is likely to require. The purpose of the next chapter is to present a clear picture of the way in which accounting information may be classified in certain definite categories and of the manner in which the accountant presents this classified information to management in well organized and clearly understandable statements.

Problems Based on Chapter I 

1. During the month of March, Dick Smith, the owner-manager of 

Smith's Drug Store performed the following acts. Consider each task carefully and decide whether it involved labor or management. 

If a management decision is involved, identify the alternatives between which Smith had to choose. Discuss the information which 

Smith needed to make an intelligent decision. What is the source of such information in each case? a. Smith buys the store building which he has been renting. He pays $15,000 cash and signs a mortgage for $25,000. k. Smith's prompt and skilful action saves the man's life. o. Smith hires a full-time bookkeeper-cashier, to save the time he devotes to these jobs. 

2. Explain the statement: "Effective accounting is a marshaling of the facts of business."

5. On April 1, J. Wallace, owner-manager of Wallace Mills has before him the following information:

It will collect $500 cash from certain rental property within the month. No other significant cash receipts are expected from business sources within the month. 

Wallace can foresee the following cash requirements during April: accounts payable coming due, $32,000; four payrolls of approximately $16,000 each; an income tax payment of $11,000 due April 

15; real estate taxes of $3,000 due on April 10. 

What is the significance of these figures to Wallace? What action will he probably take as a result of these figures?

CHAPTER II  

IT IS PROBABLY wise to acknowledge early that a business man needs many kinds of information which are outside the field of accounting. Many important facts belong in such categories as law, history, philosophy, engineering, economics, and human relations. So far as these facts do not lend themselves to measurement and expression in terms of dollars and cents, the accountant cannot recognize them in his formal picture of the business. This limitation is obviously a very significant one. Accounting does not pretend to tell all the important facts about a business; it tells only those facts that can be measured in quantitative terms, very largely in terms of dollars and cents. 

This limitation does not prevent the accountant from appreciating the importance of other kinds of information, but it does mean that he is willing to ignore certain of the nonfigure facts in order to obtain the maximum advantage from using the dollar as a common denominator or measuring device. Of course there are some accountants who let the technical aspects of dollar measurement loom so large as to become for them the sum total of all human wisdom. The limitations of accounting are stressed in this chapter to aid the student in developing into a balanced thinker on business problems while he is becoming a good accountant. 

The Purpose of Accounting 

In spite of the limitations previously mentioned, accounting can do two important tasks very effectively for any business. The first task is to picture the financial status of the business at a particular time, that is, accounting can in effect provide a snapshot of the business, which shows the important financial facts of the business at that moment. The second task is to record the important happenings or developments for a certain period, that is, accounting can in effect take a motion picture recording of the history of the business for a measured period of time. Usually the 18

significant periods of time for which we desire such histories are months or years, though some businesses also want histories for periods of one-quarter and one-half years. Quite logically the pictures of the financial status of a business which are found most useful are those taken as of the dates at the end of the history periods, whether they be by months, quarters, halves, or years.

PORTRAYING BUSINESS STATUS IN THE STATEMENT OF CONDITION 

The picture of the financial status of a business is commonly called the statement of condition, the position statement, or the balance sheet. Although the term balance sheet is still the term most widely used and the term position statement is favored in some professional circles, in this text we adhere to the term statement of condition. The statement of condition is composed of two categories. The first category embraces such items as cash in bank, merchandise, customers' accounts, equipment, buildings, and land. 

These items are all similar in that they are things owned by the business and in that they all have value which can be measured in dollars. Some of these items represent physical objects and some nonphysical-legal facts or considerations of value. Thus the words "valuable things owned" practically define the first category, if the word “things" is interpreted widely enough to include intangible as well as tangible values. The accountant uses the word asset as the common name for the items in the first category. 

At first thought, a list of all the assets of a business with their dollar values might seem to present the complete picture of the business, but such a list is in fact only half the picture. It is also necessary to know who at that moment has any claim against or right to the assets of the business. The major right to the assets is usually that of the proprietor (the partners if the business is a partnership, or the stockholders if the business is organized in the corporate form). This right may properly be referred to as owner's equity, proprietorship, or capital. Almost always, however, other important rights or claims must be shown if the picture is to be an honest and complete one. Such other claims and rights may be those of mortgagees, the holders of promissory notes, open account creditors, and even such claims as those of laborers for unpaid wages and those of utility companies for unpaid service

fees. Instead of using the double expression rights and claims, we can now use the simple word equities as the common term for all the items in this second category. 

Since all the items in the equity category are rights and claims to the assets, it follows that the total dollar measures of the equities must equal the total value of the assets. Another way of looking at the items in this second category is to recognize that the claims of all the outsiders are liabilities and are superior to the claims of the proprietors. However, when these superior claims have been satisfied, the proprietors have a right to the remaining asset value. 

Since the proprietary equity is always residual in this sense, the total of the equities must always be equal to the total of the assets. 

Figure 1 illustrates the simple statement of condition. 

CROSSROADS GENERAL STORE Statement of Condition 

December 31, 19XX

FIGURE 1. STATEMENT OF CONDITION (balanced arrangement) 

When all the status facts have been collected and their dollar measures decided upon, they can be set up in a two-column style commonly known as the account form statement of condition. 

This form emphasizes nicely the inherent equality of the two main categories of the statement and is therefore especially useful at this stage in the study of accounting. It may be thought of as expressing a formula: Assets = = Equities. For purposes of day-to-day use the statement is often set up in the form: Assets Liabilities = 

Ownership, or in still other forms, all of which really depend on and express the same basic relationships. 

The double rules under the equal totals are one of the conventions of accounting, and they indicate the agreement or balancing of the figures under which they appear. 

In Figure 2 the statement is rewritten in the alternative (vertical or report) form.

For a corporate form of ownership the statement of condition would be the same as Figure 3 (possibly with the word Incorporated added to the general heading) but with the proprietorship section reflecting the amount and value of the stock.

$20,000 5,500 $25,500

Of course, the statement might show 250 shares par $100 extended at $25,000 with only $500 of surplus, or any other combination adding up to $25,500.

Some interesting and intricate problems are involved in partnership and corporation accounting. These problems are discussed in Accounting II. For present purposes, the problems presented in this text deal with those aspects of accounting which are similar for all three forms of organization.

Further Development of the Statement of Condition 

In actual practice the statement of condition is made more useful by classifying the items in the asset and liability categories. 

The most common refinement is to sort each of these classes into its relatively current and relatively fixed items. Figure 4 shows a classified or formal statement of condition. 

Of the category assets, the cash, accounts receivable, notes receivable, inventories of merchandise, accrued incomes, prepaid expenses, and under some conditions investments are considered current assets. Such assets as buildings, land, furniture and fixtures, and trucks are considered fixed assets. The reason for this differentiation is that the current items are those which are being converted into cash in a relatively short time in the normal course of business. Thus current items are under the control of management in quite a different sense from the control exercised over the fixed assets. Management can use the cash which flows from the current assets to pay off liabilities, it can put the cash back into the merchandise-accounts receivable cycle again, it can keep cash in the actual cash status, or redirect cash to any other use which may seem wise at the time. The amounts tied up in fixed

18

assets, on the other hand, are committed to their specific uses for relatively long periods. A higher proportion of current assets thus puts management in a better position to meet current problems as they arise.

The proportion of the assets which is current becomes even more important when the liabilities are similarly divided into current and fixed groups. The current liabilities are the accounts payable, notes payable, accrued wages payable, and similar items that have to be paid relatively soon. 

Typical current assets, such as those named, usually are converted into cash within a month or two, and current liabilities usually must be settled in a similar period. For practical purposes, both assets and liabilities are considered "current" if they are to be converted into cash or paid within one year. 

It should be noted that although such assets as accounts and notes receivable and merchandise inventory and such liabilities as accounts and notes payable are usually assumed to be current, this assumption must be carefully checked. A promissory note may run for a period longer than a year, but it would certainly be misleading to include a five- or ten-year note as a current item either in the asset or in the liability section. Another item which must be watched carefully is investments. If a business owns bonds or stocks, these assets may be perfectly good current assets when they are held under conditions indicating a normal conversion into cash within a year. On the other hand, the very same stocks and bonds may be owned as pension fund investments, or as part of a plan for controlling subsidiary corporations. Such circumstances indicate relative permanence of ownership, and in these cases it would be wrong to list the investments as current assets. 

It should be noted also that such assets as a truck or a farm implement which may be a permanent asset for one business may be a current asset, merchandise, for the dealer who sells these items. Merchandise itself varies considerably in the degree of its currency. A chain store grocery may turn its merchandise into cash in as short a period as a week or ten days. An independent grocery store may take a month for each such turnover. A hardware store is usually slower still, and a jewelry store may require a period of a year or longer to turn over its merchandise. Incidentally, the jewelry store would still show its merchandise as a

current asset even though the turnover period may technically exceed the usual one-year limit. 

The significance of the current-fixed categories may now be illustrated nicely by reference to Figure 4, the Michigan Trading Company. It should be noted that the current assets, $99,450, are over seven times as great as the current liabilities, $13,630. This ratio is known as the current ratio. In this instance the current ratio seems ample and justifies the conclusion that the management will be able to meet its financial obligations nicely within the immediately foreseeable future.

Note that over half of the assets are current and that the total liabilities are less than fifteen per cent of the total equities; these proportions indicate a very stable and conservative business. The observation of these proportions and their interpretation is of course only the very first step in the making of a real analysis of the financial condition of the business. 

Additional Statement of Condition Classifications 

Although the current and fixed concepts are basic, in actual practice, they are not necessarily used as captions in statements of condition. For instance, statements of condition for manufacturing businesses commonly use the term plant assets in place of fixed assets. In this case the manufacturers are careful to classify only buildings, machinery, tools, and the like under the caption, plant assets. If long-term investments are owned by the business they are shown in a third section, probably called long-term investments or merely other assets. Frequently a major asset caption called merely investments is shown between the current asset and plant asset sections. This position implies that these items are not clearly current or fixed. It may be the best treatment available for these items if some of the investments have been owned for a long time, if they could be readily converted into cash, and if management wishes to call this fact to the attention of the readers of the statement of condition. 

Since business situations vary greatly and since it is the function of the statement of condition to portray the situation clearly, the most important generalization about the statement of condition is that its form and its captions must be selected to fit the special conditions of each case.

Specific Purpose, Form, and Degree of Detail of Statement of Condition 

The statement of condition, in addition to fitting the particular business situation, should also be tailored to the particular use that is to be made of it. Not all statements should show in full the detail available to the accountant. A statement of condition for general publication or for the stockholders' report may properly be summarized more than that drawn up for management. 

The statement drawn up to support a request for a loan from a bank should show ample detail, especially for current items. 

Some statements are summarized so highly that they provide only a single figure for plant assets; others, especially those drawn for the management, show all major categories of plant assets. 

Frequently the statement shows the original cost, the depreciation to date, and the net present book value for each item of the major plant assets. 

Among the current assets, there are similar problems concerning the degree of detail to be shown in the statement of condition. Cash may be shown as a single item or it may be classified in such categories as petty cash, checking accounts, and savings accounts. Accounts receivable may be a single figure or may show separate figures for retail accounts receivable, wholesale accounts receivable, instalment accounts receivable, and the like. Frequently, the statement of condition shows not only the accounts receivable that are legally due, but also an estimate of the uncollectable element in them and the net amount expected to be collectable. 

The statement of a manufacturing company may show a single figure for inventories, or it may show separate figures for raw materials, goods in process, finished goods, and supplies. 

All these and many other variations of the statement of condition are encountered by the student both in problems and in actual statements which he inspects. In each case he should study the particular form and determine why it is considered proper in that particular case to include the specific details shown. 

PORTRAYING THE BUSINESS HISTORY IN THE INCOME STATEMENT 

The practical day-to-day records kept by the accountant show, in considerable detail, what happens to the values of each recorded asset and equity. Much of this detail is not, however, interesting

to the business manager. Long experience indicates that of all the events which occur in a business by far the most important are those that comprise changes in the owner's equity. Thus, the recorded history of any business isolates for very special attention those items that increase and those that decrease the owner's equity. If, within a particular period, perhaps a month or a year, the increases exceed the decreases, there is a net increase in the owner's equity. Though the concepts may need to be refined later, at this stage the net increase may be thought of as the net income (or net profit) for the period. Generally speaking, the increases are "incomes" and the decreases are "expenses.' 

The formal presentation of the incomes and the expenses of a business and their net effect is known as an income statement, an operating statement, or a statement of profit and loss. The statement may take the very simple form shown in Figure 5. 

CROSSROADS GENERAL STORE Income Statement For the Year Ended December 31, 19XX

Less: Cost of Merchandise Sold..

FIGURE 5. INCOME STATEMENT 

This income or operating statement is a typical one for a small merchandising business. Naturally, the items shown would be dif-

ferent for a manufacturing business or a service business. All income statements are similar, however, in that they show sources of income and expenses so arranged as to give the reader a fair picture of the important changes which occurred during the period.

Figure 6 shows the income statement for the Michigan Trading Company as it would appear if no recognition were given to the fact that the business really consists of three ventures. 

MICHIGAN TRADING COMPANY Income Statement For the Year Ended

Cost of Goods Sold

850 1,320 740 355 80,628 $ 21,450 

Net Income for the year

FIGURE 6. SIMPLE INCOME STATEMENT 

The income statement, like the statement of condition, must be tailored to the particular business and must be drawn in sufficient detail to serve its particular purpose. The income statement presented in the report to stockholders is usually not as detailed as that prepared for the actual managers of the business. In a large business the most feasible technique is to present highly summarized figures in the income statement itself, with schedules showing the supporting detail.

The need for tailoring the income statement is encountered most frequently in those businesses which have several or many

different kinds of activity within the same concern. In these cases, the income statement should show the degree of success or failure in each of the ventures. Otherwise, if one of the ventures suffers a loss, it would reduce the income of the really successful ventures to a mediocre level. In such a case, if the facts are shown, the management often may be able to correct the difficulty and bring the weak activity into profitable operation, or if worse comes to worst, the management may decide to drop the unprofitable line. 

Since the Michigan Trading Company involves three distinct activities or ventures, each with its own unique difficulties and problems, obviously the company's income statement would be more meaningful to management if it were presented in the form of Figure 7.

The amounts previously shown in Figure 6 are now listed in the right hand or total column, with the other three columns sorting these totals into separate amounts for each of the three ventures. These individual venture columns show clearly that the truck sales venture produces the bulk of the sales, with hides and wool sales of much less importance. The cost of goods sold is shown separately for each of the three ventures and the gross margin is also shown separately. Note that the gross margin for wool is very low but that the hides and the truck ventures seem to be doing well.

Five of the expenses-wages, supplies, advertising, insurance, and depreciation-have been separated into the amounts applicable to the three ventures. The general expenses have not been divided but remain whole to be subtracted after the "departmental operating profits" have been determined. This illustration indicates a common difficulty encountered in multiple venture businesses. 

The Problem of Allocation of Income and Expense 

It is usually easy to segregate the revenues, in this case the sales, of the several ventures within a business. Some expenses may also be segregated quite easily, but some expenses are very difficult to split on any reasonable basis. In the case of the Michigan Trading 

Company, for instance, it is likely that the salesmen who handled the sales of trucks were different salesmen from those who handled the sales of hides and wool. If the company has separate sales staffs, then the wages and commissions expense properly appli-

cable to the truck sales may be separated from those of the other ventures. But if one man handled both the truck and the hides and wool sales, he must have kept a record of the time he devoted to each type of sale to provide a basis for the division of his wages. 

MICHIGAN TRADING COMPANY Income Statement For the Year Ended

FIGURE 7. CLASSIFIED INCOME STATEMENT 

The supplies chargeable to each venture could be identified at time of purchase or at time of use, largely by the nature of the item being consumed. Certainly the gas and oil used in the trucks when they were demonstrated to prospective customers would be a proper charge to the truck venture. 

The advertising expense would be apportioned between the ventures by a careful study of the nature of the advertisements. 

In the case of the Michigan Trading Company it is apparent that the hides venture was not advertised and only $100 was spent in advertising the wool venture.

Insurance expense might be somewhat harder to apportion. 

In this case the actual insurance policies must be studied to allo-

cate the expense for each venture. Insurance against certain risks, such as the typical automobile collision insurance, personal and property damage insurance, and "comprehensive" insurance on the trucks which are in stock and in process of sale, should certainly be charged to the truck venture. If insurance is carried on the truck used by the company for hauling hides and wool, the premium on that policy must be charged against those ventures. 

Other insurance, such as fire insurance on the building, should probably be charged to the three ventures in proportion to the floor space each occupies. 

The depreciation expense should be charged to each venture in accordance with the technical facts involved. If the business uses special baling equipment for hides, the depreciation on it is certainly a charge to the hides venture. If the wool division owns electric shearing equipment, the depreciation on that equipment is a proper charge to wool, and so forth. Depreciation on the building should be prorated to the three ventures in accordance with the space each occupies. 

It is very important that each allocation of expense be made to the individual venture in accordance with the way in which that venture caused the expense to be incurred or the way in which it benefited from the expense. This general rule for allocations is applied in these and similar situations involving general or joint costs. The rule is not always easy to apply, but it is the only safe rule to use as a guide.

Apparently, the accountant for the Michigan Trading Corporation could find no satisfactory basis for allocating the remaining expenses to the separate ventures. Theoretically, of course, these costs should be allocated, but if no basis can be found which conforms to the principle stated in the preceding paragraph, then it is better not to allocate these expenses. It should be noted that the accountant used the title Departmental Operating Profits to describe the amounts remaining after he had deducted the allocated expenses and that he applied the title Net Income only after all expenses had been deducted.

Management Use of the Income Statement 

The use that management makes of the statements produced by the accountant is, of course, primarily the responsibility of

management. As far as possible, however, the accountant must acquire the management viewpoint in order to produce statements that are really useful to management. Some of the principal requirements have been demonstrated in Figures 6 and 7. 

If the business contains several ventures which have separable management problems, the accounts should show each venture so clearly that the management may be adequately informed to enable it to decide what steps are necessary to cure a bad situation or to take further advantage of a good one. For instance, the Michigan Trading Company's wool venture was certainly shown by the accountant to have been unsatisfactory in the year 19XX. 

What should be done to improve the wool venture is management's problem. The results of the wool venture for the year showed an operating loss even at the departmental level. Because some of the unallocated expenses were incurred by the wool business, it is clear that the real net loss on wool is even larger than the loss shown. It appears probable from the very small gross margin on the wool that the management had a large stock of wool on hand when the price dropped suddenly. In such a case, management may have had some once-in-a-lifetime bad luck, or it may have been gambling on the price situation by allowing a large stock of wool to accumulate in its hands when in fact a company of this kind should not speculate in this way. Possibly there is a healthy and steady profit to be made by buying from the producers and selling to the commission merchants at the going prices without taking the risk of an inventory loss by holding large quantities.

If, on the other hand, the gross margin is low because competition in the area is acute and continuing and if there is no likelihood of an improvement in the situation, then perhaps the Michigan Trading Company should liquidate its wool business and expand the hides and the truck ventures in which it makes a profit. 

Nonoperating Charges 

In addition to providing a clear picture of the ventures within a business, the accountant must present certain costs or expenses that have little or no relation to operations as such. Since nonoperating charges are not under the control of management they

should be shown separately in the income statement, after the picture of the operating results has been developed. 

Interest paid on bonds and mortgages reflects the financial form established for the business by the owner, partners, or stockholders. Taxes paid, especially income taxes, are determined by external economic and political factors. For these reasons it is usually advisable to show interest and taxes, after the figure of net operating income, as subtractions from the income statement. 

Thus, net operating income measures the overall success of operations as such, but it is subject to further necessary deductions for interest and taxes paid before the final net income can be determined for the proprietors. 

When large losses are incurred, such as losses from floods, fire, or tornado, they are shown at the end of the income statement to keep these figures separate from the operations figures. 

The treatment of nonoperating charges can be illustrated by assuming a few more facts and showing how they would appear in the statement of the Michigan Trading Company (Figure 8). 

MICHIGAN TRADING COMPANY Income Statement For the Year Ended  

(See Figure 7 for operating sections of statement.) 

Net Operating Income, Year Ended June 30, 19XX. $21,450 Less: 

Loss from Flood in May 

Interest on Bonds

Net Income Before Income Taxes Federal Income Taxes Paid Net Income to Stockholders 

$7,500 480 7,980 $13,470 5,444 $ 8,026 

FIGURE 8. INCOME STATEMENT SHOWING NONOPERATING INCOME 

This practice of showing nonoperating deductions at the end of the statement should not be overdone. Smaller losses, which are expected year after year in any business, should be treated as operating costs. Usually, interest paid on current notes payable and real estate and miscellaneous taxes paid also should be treated as operating expenses, even though they are not in fact entirely under the immediate control of management.

CHAPTER III THE PATTERN OF TRANSACTIONS THE FIRST two chapters have presented a preview of accounting as employed in the two major accounting statements. It is the purpose of this chapter and the succeeding chapters to inquire into some of the mechanics involved in collecting the data upon which those statements are based. Actually, the effort to record properly business transactions is an aid in visualizing their real nature. Business transactions which the student himself has experienced or at least has heard discussed now take on new meaning and a more personal interest.

The Motion Picture Analogy 

Just as a motion picture film is a series of "stills" so the picture of a business can be reduced to a series of snapshots. The sense of motion produced by a cinema is the result of a series of small changes from one still to the next when the stills follow one another in rapid succession. The stream of business transactions may be presented in similar manner by watching the effect of each of a series of transactions on the statement of condition. 

To make this process more meaningful, the different effects may be analyzed and classified to develop a pattern. The pattern reveals that there is a definite limit to the different kinds of events that can occur in a business. 

An Illustration of Transaction Types 

As the basis for experimentation the statement of condition (balance sheet, position statement) of a small town farm implement dealer is shown in Figure 9.

Second type of transaction: 

(d) Bigge settles a creditor's account of $300 by giving the creditor a note for that amount. (e) Bigge's lawyer convinces him that his taxes owed were erroneously calculated, and that he really owes $670 instead of $470. (f) Bigge marries the daughter of the man who holds the $4,000 mortgage. His new father-in-law cancels the mortgage as a wedding gift. 

Third type of transaction: (g) Bigge collects $18 interest on the $900 bond. (h) Bigge borrows $2,500 from the bank by giving his note for that amount. (i) Bigge buys his spring stock of implements for $17,300 on account.

Fourth type of transaction: 

(j) Bigge pays his taxes, $670. (k) When he finds that it is seriously defective, Bigge returns to the vendor a $750 mower, which was purchased on account. (1) Bigge recognizes that his equipment has depreciated in value by $210.

Observe that each transaction of the first type has the same kind of effect, that is, each increases one asset and decreases another

THE PATTERN OF TRANSACTIONS 

35 asset by the same amount. Each transaction of the second type increases one equity and decreases another equity by the same amount. Each transaction of the third type increases an asset and increases an equity by the same amount. Each transaction of the fourth type decreases an asset and decreases an equity by the same amount. These four basic types of transactions can be briefly char acterized as follows: 

Type 1. asset+ asset 

Type 3. asset+ equity+

Type 4. asset- equity- 

Look at this tabulation of the types of transactions closely; notice the inherent balance of the business situation. Double entry accounting is based on this fundamental relationship. 

Further experimentation with transactions demonstrates that nothing can occur to unbalance the business situation. 

It may be a very useful exercise at this point to copy Bigge's statement of condition and to record transactions (a) through (1) therein by the actual erasing and changing of figures. When these changes are made correctly, the final statement appears as shown in Figure 10. Cash

In one sense the four types of transactions previously stated give the complete story of everything that can possibly occur in a business; they exhaust the possible types of accounting entries.

THE PATTERN OF TRANSACTIONS 

Practically, however, we must recognize that many actual transactions in business are combinations of two or more of the basic types. For instance, the most common transaction in business is the sale of merchandise for cash at a profit. In Bigge's implement business, if a plow which had cost $75 is sold for $100 cash, three accounts would be affected: Cash increases $100; Implement Inventory decreases $75; and Owner's Equity increases $25. This transaction can be visualized as a combination of a type 1 transaction for $75 (Cash+, Implement Inventory—) and a type 3 for $25 (Cash+, Owner's Equity+). This transaction may be separated into the two simple types to demonstrate that it is a combination of types and that the entries leave the statement of condition in balance. The practical way of recording this transaction, however, is in the three-item form as previously discussed. 

Note that the customary sale on account at a profit is similar to the cash sale at a profit; the only difference is that, instead of 

Cash, Customers' Accounts are increased. The sale of an item for less than its cost would increase Cash (or Customers' Accounts), decrease Implement Inventory, and decrease Owner's Equity.

In most normal businesses a great many transactions affect the owner's equity account or capital, and we have already noted in the previous chapter that such transactions are especially interesting to the owner. We can visualize the capital account as a battleground in which the increases and decreases (that is, the incomes and expenses) are constantly battling for supremacy. We can compare the balance of the Owner's Equity at the end of a period with its balance at the beginning of the period to see the results of the battle. The proprietor wants much more information, however, than the bare fact of his total net income. Obviously, we are going to have to develop techniques for preserving all this detail regarding incomes and expenses. 

One caution deserves notice at this point. The proprietor may withdraw cash or other valuables from the business for his private use, or he may put additional money or property into the business. His capital decreases or increases by these amounts, and we must be careful not to confuse these transactions with his business expenses and incomes, or we may mislead the proprietor concerning the real profitableness of his business. For a further discussion of this point see Chapter V.

THE PATTERN OF TRANSACTIONS

CHAPTER IV  

ALTHOUGH the erasing-and-changing method of recording transactions shown in Chapter III may be used to handle any possible transaction, it would obviously be impracticable to use it for a large volume of transactions because it would be too slow and because the work could not be checked if an error should occur. 

The method is also impractical since it gives final results for each item without preserving the history of the change, and the history is important for some items, especially for the owner's equity. 

THE NECESSITY OF IMPROVED TECHNIQUES 

The first practical technique for handling large volumes and preserving the history of transactions resulted from the observation that only two changes can ever take place in any accounting item on the statement of condition. It can either increase or decrease; nothing else can happen to it. Thus some device for recording the plus and minus changes in each item on the statement of condition is essentially the first step toward a practical system for keeping records. In actual accounting practice it is necessary to provide space for explanation, date, reference to sources of data, and the like for each account. Most of these special devices can be omitted in this chapter, however, in order to concentrate attention on basic relationships. 

Setting Up the T-Account Ledger 

Of course, the spaces between the items in the statement of condition itself might be expanded to show any additions or subtractions or both. Since the items vary so greatly in their activity, however, it is desirable to provide more flexibility than the statement of condition would allow. Another form of record, different from the statement of condition but closely related to it, must be used. The close relationship is preserved by recognizing in- 39

dividually each of the items in the statement as well as by maintaining the basic group relationships among them. This form is called the T-account ledger. In it a two-column space or T is provided for each item to permit the recording of additions and subtractions. The original amounts and the additions to all assets are placed on the left side of the T, the subtractions on the right. 

The original amounts and the additions to all the equities are placed on the right side and the subtractions on the left side. 

Figure 11 illustrates a ledger set up with the original items, marked by asterisks (*), followed by the same transactions used previously for the Bigge Farm Implement business in Figure 9, Chapter III. 

The figures for the transactions have been keyed with the letters (a), (b), (c), and so forth. To study this illustration effectively, find the two related items for each transaction, reconstruct the transaction in your own mind; then, if necessary, check the descriptions of the transactions following Figure 9 in Chapter III. 

The Left and Right Equality 

Careful inspection discloses the fact that each one of these transactions, no matter what its type, involves the left side of one account and the right side of another. This fact is the result of the way in which the plus and minus sign scheme is devised, and it provides a very useful check on mechanical accuracy. If your original thinking on a transaction should miscarry and you find you have two left entries or two right entries, you know that your analysis is incorrect and that you must reconsider the problem before the error becomes buried in the accounts. This left and right equality holds true even for the combination entries. When 

Bigge sells a plow which cost $75 for $100 cash, the entry is made as a $100 left in Cash, a $75 right in Implement Stock, and a $25 right in Owner's Equity. Thus, in any transaction, the amounts entered on the left side of accounts are equal to the amounts entered on the right side of accounts. 

Traditionally, accountants use the words debit and credit instead of left and right in speaking of the entering of transactions. 

As used in modern accounting these two words, debit and credit, mean only left and right. It is without purpose and actually dangerous to try to make them mean more than that. They do not

have the meanings "good" and "bad," "favorable” and “unfavorable,” “in” and “out," or any other of the connotations sometimes read into them. Since, as previously pointed out, the left entries always equal the right entries, the well-known principle may now be declared: debits equal credits!

It may be useful to point out in tabular form the relationships between the basic groups of accounts, the sign scheme, and the words debit and credit, although this involves nothing new. debits (left side entries) increase assets debits (left side entries) decrease equities credits (right side entries) decrease assets credits (right side entries) increase equities Since the equities include both the liabilities—that is, the creditors' equities—and the owner's equity, the impact of the debits and credits on each of these subdivisions is the same. In other words: debits decrease liabilities and owner's equity credits increase liabilities and owner's equity

Balancing the Ledger Accounts 

At the end of an accounting period it is desirable to summarize the information in each account by calculating its balance and by showing this balance in a single figure. Thus the ending balance for one period becomes the beginning balance for the next period. The end-of-the-period relationship is neatly shown by ruling and bringing down the balances of the active accounts. 

Whether the simple T-accounts are being used in problems or whether the more formal ledger accounts shown later in this chapter are being used, the method of balancing follows a more or less standardized pattern as shown in Figure 12 for Bigge's 

Farm Implement business.

It should be noted that asset balances are brought down on the debit side and equity balances on the credit side, in accordance with the sign scheme. Furthermore, inactive accounts, such as 

Land and Buildings which have only their original items, do not need to be ruled, totaled, and balanced. Note also that two of the accounts, Taxes Owed and Mortgage Payable, actually have no balances to bring down since their debits and credits are equal.

It is thought to be the best practice, however, to rule and bring down the balances of all accounts which have shown activity during the period, and this practice is required for the problems in this text. 

The Account in Actual Practice 

As previously stated, actual accounting practice requires some provision for an explanation, a date, and a source reference for each account. Space for these identifications must be provided for each account in both the debit and the credit columns. In addition, each account should be provided with room for expansion because many entries may have to be made in it. Practically, each account requires a page for itself. Instead of the single sheet used for the skeleton T-accounts appearing in Figure 12, the ledger is usually a thick book, either bound or loose-leaf, with one or more pages for each account, or it may be a vertical file of cards in a tray or drawer.

The actual arrangement of columns and spaces may vary considerably, but two general types are more or less standard. These types are illustrated in Figures 13 and 14. 

The standard ledger form of account is obviously the T-account with practical additions. It, or some slight variation of it, is used in most manually operated ledgers. The "R" in the narrow column stands for reference. Frequently an "F" is found in this column instead of an "R"; it has the same basic significance since it is derived from the initial letter of the word "folio" (meaning page). Either letter, R or F, refers to the page number of the source of the entry.

The debit, credit, and balance form of account is commonly used for systems in which the entries are made by machine, because the machine can be made to calculate and print the balance after every entry with very little additional effort. With this form of account, it is not necessary to total the accounts at the end of the accounting period when the bookkeeper is usually pressed for time. The form can, of course, be used for a manual operation, but for most accounts the showing of the balance after every entry represents extra effort and is a waste of time, since management is seldom interested in detailed balances. For certain accounts,

such as those with individual customers and creditors, it might be desirable to watch the balances as they develop. This special application of the debit, credit, and balance form may well be used even in the manual system.

Figures 15 and 16 illustrate the use of the two forms with actual figures as they might appear for the account Investments. 

The references in both cases are to the particular pages and to the names of the journals from which the figures came. The journals are discussed in Chapters VIII, IX, and X. 

Note that the standard form which assumed manual entries (Figure 15) shows small penciled trial totals at the foot of each amount column. Since actual debit and credit entries are made in ink these small penciled totals are not likely to be confused with or read as additional debits and credits. The trial total of the credits must be taken in the process of determining the balance of the account. When the balance has been determined, it is written on the credit side with the notation "balance down"; the lines indicating the formal addition are drawn exactly across from each other, the formal totals are entered, the double lines are drawn to indicate the equality of the totals, and the balancing amount with the new date is brought down on the debit side. 

Note that the single rules extend only across the amount columns whereas the double rules extend across all columns except the explanation column. This process of ruling and bringing down the balance obviously parallels the process described earlier for use in T-accounts. It is sometimes thought of as a mere formality, but it is a very useful formality because it is a standard practice which every accountant understands. It is another of the small conventions that keep the civilized world running smoothly. 

If the account illustrated were an equity account, it would have a credit balance instead of the debit balance shown in the 

Investment Account (Figure 15). If a credit balance were shown, the positions of the balancing amount above the lines and the balance brought down would be reversed, but the basic idea would not be changed. The purpose of this formality is to summarize in one figure the figures shown by the account. 

Figure 16 illustrates the debit, credit, and balance form of the account used in a machine operation. The same transactions of the Investment Account are repeated and, of course, result in

The placing of the reference and date columns is shifted from the position in which they are shown in Figure 14, and an extra column called “old balance pick up" is included in the technique used by the particular machine. The operator posting an item must first enter the previous balance of the account on the keyboard of the machine to be printed in the old balance pick up column of the account. Thus, an error can be discovered by comparing the old balance pick up figure with the actual figure for the previous balance shown in the balance column one line higher up.

In both Figure 15 and Figure 16 the explanation columns have been used rather sparingly, and the explanations are rather cryptic. Brief explanations are fairly representative of actual practice, and they are probably desirable, since there is seldom any need for detail when looking at the account. If, in any specific instance, someone questions the nature of a particular debit or credit, complete information can be obtained by checking the source of the entry through the page number in the reference column.

Since most of the problems in this book are relatively short and simple, it is not necessary to carry complete date, reference, and explanation for the entries made. The student can, therefore, save his time and concentrate his attention on the basic relationship by using the simple T-account. In actual accounting practice, however, the complete reference, the date, and occasionally the explanation are all necessary for every entry that is made in the ledger.

The Trial Balance 

In an earlier section of this chapter, the inherent balance of the accounting situation was mentioned. This balance results from the sign-scheme used and from the very nature of the asset-equity relationship. If every entry is correct, the statement of condition at the end of any accounting period will balance automatically. 

Since the entries are made by fallible human beings, however, and especially since the volume of transactions grows very large, the probability of error is such that it must be recognized. Every reasonable precaution is taken to guard against errors and to locate them promptly, if they are made in spite of the precautions. Later

50

in this text a variety of procedures devised to obtain debit and credit accuracy are discussed.

One of the principal safeguards of such accuracy is the trial balance that the bookkeeper takes periodically, usually at the end of each month. The trial balance involves a listing of all the accounts in the ledger; their amount balances are sorted into two columns, one for debit balances and one for credit balances. The trial balance usually lists the accounts in the order in which they appear in the ledger.

Since only asset and equity accounts in the ledger are dealt with in this chapter, the trial balance contains only these accounts and hence is similar to the statement of condition in content, though not in form. As additional types of accounts are introduced in subsequent chapters (accounts for expenses, revenues, contra accounts, and so forth), these accounts also are shown in the trial balance which no longer exhibits so close a resemblance to the statement of condition. Although the "error-catching" function of the trial balance is stressed in this chapter, its other uses are not inconsistent with accuracy-testing. 

Even though the accuracy of the debits and the credits is proved by the trial balance, this fact cannot be interpreted to mean that the work has all been done correctly. For instance, if the wrong account is debited or credited, the trial balance is still balanced even though the error may be quite serious. The trial balance also fails to indicate error if a transaction is entirely omitted, if it is entered twice, or if it is entered for a wrong amount both as to debit and credit. Thus, emphasis is again placed on the necessity for developing a series of devices to guard against error.

Figure 17 illustrates the trial balance for Bigge's Farm Implements business after entering transactions (a) through (1) that follow Figure 9, in Chapter III. 

The two accounts which no longer have active balances might have been omitted entirely. The inclusion of these accounts with zero balances, as shown in Figure 17, is useful if this trial balance is likely to be compared with previous or subsequent trial balances having these items. Sometimes trial balances are taken by showing both debit and credit totals for each account. Such a trial balance is equally effective in proving the debit-credit bal-

ance. For the sake of uniformity it is suggested that all trial balances required to work the problems in the text be taken in the form illustrated in Figure 17, that is, that they be the trial balance of balances, rather than the trial balance of debit and credit totals. This method is known as the "by-balances" rule. Cash
