FIGURE 17. TRIAL BALANCE Locating Errors in the Trial Balance 

If the trial balance fails to balance, the first step in finding the error is to determine the exact amount of the difference between the debit and credit totals. If this is an error of 1 in any column (that is 14, $10, or $10,000 or the like) it is probably an error in addition or subtraction either in the trial balance itself or in one of the accounts.

If the error is a distinctive amount, such as the $37.98 which occurred in the problem, it is likely that the error is simply the omission of either the debit or credit half of the entry.

If the error is an even number, that number should be divided by two, and the resulting quotient should be sought in the figures of the problem. This rule develops from the fact that the entry of any figure on the wrong side of an account (that is, as a debit

instead of as a credit or the reverse) will result in an error equal to twice the misplaced figure. If an entry which should have been a debit of $230 to Cash is accidentally put on the credit side of the Cash account, the final balance of the account and of the trial balance will be wrong by twice $230, that is, by $460. Since this error occurs frequently, the technique for locating it is a helpful one to recall.

If the error cannot be located through the use of the preceding suggestions, the amount of the error should be divided by nine. 

If the amount of the error can be divided by nine an even number of times, it is likely that the error resulted from the transposition of digits. Therefore, it is likely that in the handling of the problem, the digits of some figure were transposed, for instance an amount was written $9,754.29 when the correct figure was $9,574.29. This particular error would produce a variance of $180 which may be divided by nine to give a quotient of $20 even. 

A special kind of transposition is the misplacing of a decimal point. Assume that $1 is entered instead of $10, or $50 instead of $5,000. A one-place misplacement of the decimal gives an error evenly divisible by nine. A two-place misplacement gives an error evenly divisible by nine with a quotient which is then divisible by 11. For example, the entering of $5,000 instead of $50 produces an error of $4,950. This amount divided by nine gives $550, which, divided by 11, gives $50. This method of testing for an error indicates the digits (50) which have been mishandled, and the work may be checked for the figures 50 or 5,000 whenever they occur. There are other refinements of the procedure for locating errors which are occasionally useful, but the methods previously mentioned are the principal ones. 

Of course, when two or more errors are compounded, the net resulting error is usually not indicative of the cause or the location of the difficulty. Therefore, work should be checked so carefully and so frequently that the errors may be found one by one, without any likelihood of compounding them. 

The Informal Trial Balance 

When a trial balance must be taken for the sole purpose of proving the mechanical accuracy of the accounts, an adding machine tape may be run directly from the ledger without the

labor of listing all the accounts and their balances. This method, however, is used more often in practical bookkeeping than in the problems in the text. When a trial balance is required in the directive for a problem, the answer must contain a formal listing. 

Problems Based on Chapter IV

CHAPTER V  

THEORETICALLY, we might continue to use the capital account (owner's equity) itself, as we have been doing, for all of the transactions affecting capital. If we use the capital account in this way, we must label each item as it is entered, and then sort them out at the end of the accounting period to provide the totals that the owner needs regarding each kind of income and expense. 

This procedure would be very cumbersome; it can be avoided by classifying the items of income and of expense as they occur. 

On the plus side of the capital account, therefore, a T-account is set up for each kind of income, and on the minus side a Taccount is set up for each kind of expense. The debit and credit (that is, left and right) scheme is not changed at all. An entry which would have been placed on the credit (right, plus) side of the capital account is now placed on the credit side of the appropriate income account. Similarly, an entry which would have been placed on the debit (left, minus) side of the capital account is now placed on the debit side of the appropriate expense account. 

It is important to think of the income and expense accounts as the right and left sides respectively of the capital account, because this relationship ties them to the basic sign scheme. Visualizing the income and expense accounts as temporary subdivisions of the right and left sides of the capital account is also an aid in the next step-the "closing entries."

An illustration will clarify the technique used in setting up the revenue and expense accounts and in making the current entries. 

The accompanying illustration, Figure 18, shows the T-account ledger of Marshall Rice, Incorporated. The figures marked with asterisks are balances as of the beginning of the accounting period. 

The following current transactions have been entered, and can be traced by the key letters. (a) Merchandise is purchased on account, $500. (b) A week's wages are paid, $350. 57

Note that the procedure used in making these entries in the expanded ledger is not basically different from the procedure used in the previous chapter in which only the statement of condition accounts are utilized. In Figure 18, the three sales profits, the interest income, and the rent revenue are credited to their respective accounts whereas, in Figures 12 and 17 of the previous chapter, they have to be credited directly to the owner's equity account. 

Likewise in this illustration (Figure 18) the wages, advertising, interest, and office expenses are debited to their respective accounts instead of being debited to the capital account. 

It should be obvious that, in active business, credit entries constitute the primary flow into the revenue accounts with the result that these accounts normally have credit balances. The revenue accounts occasionally may receive debits for the correction of errors or other unusual situations, and at the end of each period a debit is made to close each revenue account. Closing entries are discussed later in this chapter. Similarly the expense accounts receive a preponderance of debits, their only credits being adjustments of one kind or another and closing entries. In spite of the one-sidedness of these accounts, the accountant uses the full debit and credit form for them with the result that the regular debit and credit technique provides for every eventuality. 

Sales and Cost of Sales Accounts 

Instead of handling each sale as a compound (three-item) entry, each sale is separated into two entries by using two new accounts,

Sales, and Cost of Sales (or Cost of Goods Sold) instead of the 

Sales Profits account. The amount of the transaction at the sales price is debited to Cash or Accounts Receivable and credited to the Sales account. At the same time an entry is made for the amount of the cost of the goods, debiting the Cost of Sales account and crediting the Merchandise account. The Sales account is usually shown among the revenue accounts under the right side of the Capital account and the Cost of Sales is shown among the expense accounts under the left side. 

Closing Entries 

If we visualize the income and expense accounts as they would appear at the end of a month, we will see each income account with one or more (sometimes a great many) credits, and each expense account with a number of debits. When we total each of these income and expense accounts we are in a position to prepare the income statement for the proprietor, using our best judgment to match the incomes and expenses in the most meaningful manner, as discussed in Chapter II. Naturally the proprietor is anxious to learn the results of the period's operations, therefore this task is given a high priority at the end of the accounting period.

In addition to bringing the incomes and expenses together in the income statement, the accountant brings them together formally in the ledger itself. This process acts as a check on the calculations for the income statement. After the closing entries have been made, the accounts are in condition to receive the transactions of the next accounting period with no danger of confusion occurring between the transactions of the two periods. The key to the closing process is the setting up of a new account called Expense and Revenue whose sole function is to provide a meeting place for the revenues and expenses. 

A refinement in terminology may well be useful at this point. 

Heretofore we have used the terms income and revenue as though they were synonymous, and in fact they are often so used in common parlance. However, "income" should be limited to those increases in proprietorship which are net, or virtually so, at the time they are received; whereas "revenue" more properly describes such increases as "sales," "rents received," "service fees

received," and the like which are "gross" increases—in the sense that they are normally accompanied by certain expenses against which they must be matched before the real net effect on proprietorship is arrived at. For this reason the meeting place account which we use in our closing entries is designated Expense and Revenue. Many of the credits in this account are, in the strict sense, revenues rather than incomes. In actual bookkeeping practice the terms "Expense and Income," "Profit and Loss," and even "Loss and Gain" are encountered, and no harm is done so long as the accounts are properly handled. In the long run, of course, better understanding will result from using better terminology. 

Since bringing the revenues and expenses together is done in the ledger, it is desirable to maintain the debit and credit equality in each entry. This equality is maintained by debiting each income account, including Sales, for the amount of its total (or balance if it happens to have any debits) and by crediting the Expense and Revenue account for the same amount. Similarly, a credit is entered to each expense account, including Cost of Sales, for its total (or balance) and a debit to the Expense and Revenue account. These entries have the effect of closing the revenue accounts and the expense accounts and of transferring their balances to the meeting place account Expense and Revenue. 

At this point all the revenues and expenses are in the Expense and Revenue account. The two sides of this account are totaled; the difference between the two totals is net income if the revenues are greater than the expenses, or net loss if the expenses exceed the revenues. This net income or net loss is transferred to the owner's equity account to show the net change in capital resulting from the operations of the period.

Much of this detailed work, both the current entering and the making of the closing entries, may be regarded as “bookkeeping." 

The fact is, however, that most good accountants are very neat and exact in their bookkeeping if no one is assigned to do this detailed work for them. It is also true that good bookkeepers must have a reasonable knowledge of accounting-there really is no need to draw a clear line of demarcation between the two areas of activity.

The accompanying illustration of the ledger of John Gannon, Cattle Dealer, Figure 19, shows both a series of current entries

keyed by small numbers, and the closing entries keyed by letters. 

Generally, it is wise to take a trial balance after all the current entries for the period have been made and before proceeding to the work of the closing entries. Figure 20 illustrates a pre-closing trial balance for John Gannon.

Notice that this form adheres strictly to the "by-balances" rule. 

One of the income accounts, Interest Income had a $5 debit for an adjustment. This account is shown at its net balance, $85, in the trial balance. Similarly the Advertising Expense account had a credit of $25, and therefore its balance of $520 was shown instead of its total of $545.

Gannon's ledger (Figure 19) provides an opportunity to review the method of ruling and balancing the accounts. Notice that the active asset and equity accounts have been ruled and their balances brought down as described in Chapter IV. Notice that the ruling of the expense and revenue accounts follows the same general form, but that these accounts show no balances below the double lines since their balances have been transferred to the Expense and Revenue account. Notice, too, that the balance of the Expense and Revenue account, $470, has been closed to John Gannon, Capital. The Capital account is ruled and balanced in the same way that other equity accounts are. This technique provides a clear picture of what has happened to the Capital account during the accounting period. To the beginning balance of $41,- 

980, the net income for the period, $470, has been added, producing a new balance of $42,450, which now takes its place as the beginning balance for the next accounting period. 

If another trial balance of the ledger were taken in its postclosing state, it would show zero balances for, or would simply omit, all the expense and revenue accounts. 

Graphic Presentation of Closing Entries 

The making of the closing entries is a simple routine to the trained accountant, but it is often difficult for beginners. With this in mind, the closing of John Gannon's accounts is represented in Figure 21 in graphic or flow chart form to parallel the closing entries shown with specific amounts in Figure 19.

67

John Gannon, Capital and crediting Cash. The record made by these entries to the Capital account would enable Gannon to see clearly what had occurred in his capital-an increase by the amount of the net income and a decrease by the amount of his withdrawals.

If the proprietor makes frequent withdrawals of cash (or of merchandise) for his own use, it may be desirable to set up a separate account called John Gannon, Drawing, in which to collect his withdrawals rather than to enter many small items in the capital account itself. Occasionally, such an account is called John 

Gannon, Personal. Sometimes, the account is used to compare the earnings with the drawings, by closing the net income into this account instead of into the Capital account. The Drawing, or 

Personal account, in any case must be regarded as a part of the Capital account. If the Drawing account has a credit balance, the balance is an addition to capital as shown in the Capital account. 

If the Drawing account has a debit balance, the balance is a subtraction from capital as shown in the Capital account. If a statement of condition is drawn up while the drawing account has a balance, that balance should be shown added to or subtracted from the Capital account in the owner's equity section of the statement of condition. The use of the owner's drawing account is presented graphically in Figure 22. 

The owner's withdrawals should never be confused with the expenses of the business, although he may speak of his withdrawals as salary. Even if he withdraws stated amounts at regular intervals, the amounts are still drawings, that is, reductions of capital, rather than salary expense. It is, of course, proper to have a Salary 

Expense account to record salaries paid to other persons. 

If net income has been closed to the drawing account and if the proprietor decides to leave part of the earnings in the business permanently in order to expand the business, this decision is shown in the accounts by transferring that amount from his Drawing account to the Capital account. If he wishes merely to retain earnings from a good year to equalize his drawings in subsequent poor years, the balance is carried forward in the drawing account.

Chapter V, Problem 3 (continued) 

The ledger of M. Walley, Sales & Service Garage, is shown as it stood on August 21, when you took charge as accountant. You are to enter the remaining transactions for August and to follow the additional directives given below. a. New cars which cost $8,000 are sold for $10,000; $3,000 cash, with notes for the remainder b. Used cars "on the books" at $1,700 are sold for $2,000. Terms: $1,000 cash, and notes for the remainder c. Salesmen's commissions paid in cash, $1,400 d. Garage service revenue for the week, on account, $7,000 e. Parts used in the week's repair work cost $2,200 f. Advertising expense paid, $1,100 g. Gas bill paid for the month, $80 h. Garage mechanics wages paid for one week, $3000 i. Walley collected $6,300 on account and $5,000 principal plus $200 interest on notes j. Walley paid $10,000 on accounts payable k. Wages accrued for the last three days of the month are $500 

1. It is estimated that the taxes accrued for the month are $500 (This amount is in addition to the $400 already paid for August.) m. A review of insurance policies indicates insurance expired during the month, $1,450 n. Depreciation for the month is 1% of book value of equipment. 

Directives: 

(1) After entering the transactions, draw up Walley's income statement for the month in order to develop the operating net income for each of the three major divisions of the business, taking into consideration the following facts: (a) Salesmen's commissions apply: $6,000 to new car sales and $3,000 to used car sales. (b) Advertising is considered to be applicable equally to new and used car sales (none to garage service). (c) Insurance is applicable $500 to new cars, $200 to used cars, and $750 to garage service. (d) Walley follows the suggestion of the X Motor Company, whose agency he holds, in charging office, utilities, taxes, depreciation, and miscellaneous expense all against the garage service division of the business. (2) Draw up Walley's statement of condition as of August 31. (3) Close the accounts as though this were the end of the year. Rule, total, and bring down the balances where appropriate to place the accounts in condition for the beginning of the next period. (4) Discuss Walley's management and the propriety of the accounting system.

CHAPTER VI MERCHANDISE TRANSACTIONS AND RELATED PRACTICAL PROBLEMS 

IN PREVIOUS Chapters, all transactions for sales are assumed to be fairly large transactions. Under such conditions it is possible to ascertain and record the cost of the goods sold at the time of each sale. The gross profit is determined and credited to the Sales 

Profits account, or else the cost of the goods sold is separated from the merchandise inventory account and recorded in the Cost of Goods Sold account at the time of each sale, which is really a constructive recognition of the profit. This pattern is desirable for the entry of such items as farm implements, television sets, and cattle.

If, however, we are doing the accounting for a drug store, grocery store, or other business in which the unit of sale is small, obviously it is nearly impossible, and certainly impracticable, to distribute the credit for each sale directly to the cost and profit components or even to recognize the cost of each sale in the Cost of Goods Sold account. For the high volume, repetitious transactions of the retail store, we must prevent even the slightest unnecessary effort, because even a fraction of a minute when multiplied by millions wastes significant amounts of time and money. 

The Accounting Pattern for Small Unit-Large Volume Sales 

The problem of accounting for small unit-large volume sales has a practical solution which is generally used in retailing. The solution is to record only the amount of the sale at the time the sale is made and to postpone the calculation of the cost of the goods sold and the gross margin until the end of the month or other accounting period. The entry for a cash sale is simply a debit to cash and a credit to the account "Sales." The entry for a sale on account is a debit to Customers' Accounts and a credit to Sales. The Sales account is usually classified as a revenue account although it is clearly a mixed account containing both the profit 77

on sales element and the cost of goods sold element which is a credit to the asset account merchandise. At the end of the period, the total of the sales as shown by the Sales account is separated into its two component elements and the proper proportion is closed to the credit of the merchandise account at that time. 

The separation of the Sales account into its two elements requires the calculation of the cost of the goods sold by subtracting the value of the goods left on hand at the end of the period from the total of (1) the goods on hand at the beginning plus (2) the goods purchased during the period. To ascertain the value of the goods on hand at the end of the period, it is customary to take a physical count of the quantities actually on hand, pricing each item, extending, and totaling. The procedure is known as “taking inventory." This arduous task must be performed carefully and correctly, for any error in the inventory causes an error in the profit on sales and in the resulting figures for net operating profit and net income, which are reported to management. 

The Income Statement in Retailing 

In the typical retail business the relationship of sales to beginning and ending inventories and to goods purchased during the period are usually considered sufficiently significant to be reported in the income statement. Thus, the typical form of income statement used for retailers is illustrated in Figure 23; it does not begin with the gross margin on sales (profit on sales); instead it begins with sales.

The term "gross margin on sales" is customary (instead of merely margin or profit on sales). This term calls attention to the fact that there are still other costs or expenses which must be deducted before the real measure of success, that is, the net operating profit, is shown. Some accountants refuse to use the word profit, even when it is modified by the word gross, at this point in the statement because they fear that it has a misleading implication. 

They insist that the term margin is much less likely to be misleading. Both terms doubtless will remain in use for a long time. 

Accounting for Purchases and Freight-In 

It should be noted that the income statement for the Chet Miller Pharmacy gave separate figures for the inventory at the

beginning of the period and for the subsequent purchases. To make certain that both of these figures are clearly available at statement time, it is customary to use separate accounts in the ledger for inventory and purchases, although it should still be recognized that they are both parts of the merchandise record which was shown previously in the single merchandise account. 

When the account "Purchases" is used in this way, it refers to purchases of merchandise intended for sale and not (1) to purchases of expense materials intended to be consumed in the business or (2) to purchases of equipment, machinery, and the like, intended to be more or less permanent assets of the business. 

CHET MILLER PHARMACY Income Statement For the Month of August 19XX 

Sales $11,350

Cost of Goods Sold: 

Inventory, August 1 .... $ 8,395

August Purchases 

6,752 $15,147 

Inventory August 31 8,554 6,593 

Gross Margin on Sales........ $ 4,757 

FIGURE 23. INCOME STATEMENT OF A RETAIL BUSINESS 

When freight or express or cartage charges of a substantial amount are paid on merchandise purchases, these amounts are considered an additional cost of the merchandise. They may be debited directly to the Purchases account or they may be debited to an account called "Freight-In" or "Freight-on-Purchases." In the ledger this account should be shown near to the Purchases account and in the income statement its total should be added to purchases. When the amounts paid for freight are inconsequential, they are frequently classed as an expense. Although this classification is not theoretically correct, it may be justified on the ground of expediency.

If freight charges are a significant amount and are added to purchases either directly or through the use of a separate Freight-In

account, a proper amount of freight must also be included in the purchase cost when valuing the goods on hand in taking the inventory. Ideally, the freight element should be added separately to the cost of each item in the inventory in accordance with the actual freight charge paid on the item when it was purchased. In the case of inventories involving many small items, however, such detailed accuracy requires an unreasonable amount of work. The practical compromise is to list the separate items in the inventory at purchase prices, to total them, and then to add a percentage of the total for freight. The proper amount to add for freight is determined by finding the ratio between the total amount of freight paid during the period and the total amount of purchases for the period. Unless there is reason to believe that the “mix” of the kinds of goods in the inventory is different from the "mix” in the period's purchases, this method provides a satisfactory amount to be included for freight in the final inventory figure. 

Returns and Allowances

Not uncommonly goods that have been sold are returned by the customer, and sometimes goods that have been purchased are returned to the creditor. The entries are made either in the same accounts as exact reversals of the original entries for the sale or the purchase or in separate accounts called "Sales Returns" and "Purchase Returns" to indicate the nature of the entries more clearly. If the buyer does not actually return the goods but is given a concession in the price that he must pay, the transaction has somewhat the same effect. In this case we speak of sales or purchase "allowances" rather than returns. Frequently the same accounts are used for the allowances as for the actual returns. In this case the accounts should be named Sales Returns and Allowances and Purchase Returns and Allowances. Illustrative entries for returns and allowances are shown in Figure 25 later in this chapter. 

Some reasons for returns and allowances are misrepresentation of the quality of the goods by the salesman, delivery of the wrong goods, damage to the goods while in transit, and delivery delayed beyond the time at which the goods were needed. These reasons are sufficiently important to require that the facts be called to the attention of management in no uncertain terms. Returns and

allowances are brought to the attention of management in the income statement as illustrated in Figure 24. 

CHET MILLER PHARMACY Income Statement For the Month of August 19XX

Gross Margin on Sales.

FIGURE 24. INCOME STATEMENT EMPHASIZING RETURNS AND ALLOWANCES 

If we contrast Chet Miller's income statement as previously shown, (sales and purchases net, Figure 23) with the statement in Figure 24, we can see that the more complete form gives the owner-manager information which he ought to watch closely. 

If sales returns and allowances remain at a low figure which the owner considers reasonable, he may be satisfied, but if they show a significant increase, he is warned that difficulties are developing in the sales picture. He is then in a position to investigate the difficulty and, if possible, to correct it. Perhaps the returns occur because a new salesman is trying too hard and is overselling merchandise, which is returned later. Possibly merchandise is being damaged by careless delivery techniques, or perhaps a new line of merchandise is not as satisfactory as it was expected to be. In a very small business the proprietor would notice and correct many of these difficulties, but as businesses grow, management must rely more and more upon the accounting system to uncover such facts and bring them to its attention.

The factors causing purchase returns and allowances to grow unreasonably are quite as serious and important to management as those causing sales returns and allowances to grow. If the unsatisfactory purchases are concentrated in the goods from one particular supplier, it may be desirable to shift to another source of supply. If investigation shows that the purchases are returned because the order did not specify carefully enough the goods desired, the error should be traced to the employee responsible and he should be given more effective instructions to prevent a recurrence of the errors. If incoming goods are being damaged in transit, perhaps the transportation service should be shifted to some other carrier.

In these instances, the facts are reported in the accounts, and management is warned that difficulties are developing. Management still has the task of locating the difficulties and correcting the condition. This task may require special analytical studies of the situation and possibly the accountant can be useful in making such further studies. He has discharged his principal obligation, however, when his accounting system directs attention unmistakably to the initial fact that "something is wrong." 

Cash Discounts on Sales and Purchases

In some lines of trade it is customary to offer the customer a "cash discount" of one, two, or three percent if he will pay for his purchases within five or ten days instead of taking the customary thirty or sixty days to pay.

The discount terms which the seller grants are shown on the invoice, and technically, they are a part of the sales contract. Terms shown as: 2/10, n/30 (to be read as two ten, net thirty) would permit the customer to subtract 2% from the invoice price if he pays within ten days of the date of the invoice; otherwise, he is expected to pay the full amount in thirty days. It should be noted that the terms 2/10, n/30, if strictly interpreted, really amount to 2% for twenty days or an annual discount rate of roughly 36%. 

It is easy to see, therefore, why practically all customers take such discounts. It may be advisable to borrow money from the bank at any normal commercial rate to take such discounts. 

In some areas and some lines of business the time limit for the discount period is not enforced strictly, and customers take ad-

vantage of the discount although they send their checks some days after the discount period has expired. Some firms make a practice of paying all bills on the 10th and the 25th of the month and take all discounts shown on the invoices even though some of the discount periods have expired. Originally these discounts were thought of as closely similar to interest. In other words, the seller "paid" interest to collect his money promptly, and the buyer "earned" interest by paying promptly. However, the extreme rate involved and the lax way in which the terms are applied in modern usage cause some accountants to regard cash discounts as just another price concession and therefore to subtract them from the gross sales and purchases in the income statement, just as the returns and allowances are subtracted. Other accountants still show purchase discounts and sales discounts in the income statement as though they were comparable respectively to interest income and interest expense. Probably both of these methods of handling discounts will be encountered for a long time in the income statements shown to banks by their customers. The accountant should insist on consistent practice in successive periods so that the statements may be truly comparable. 

Trade Discounts on Sales and Purchases 

Not to be confused with cash discounts, discussed in the previous section, are trade discounts which are revisions of the list or catalogue prices available without any reference to the time of payment. Such discounts may be used by a wholesaler to change the effective prices of goods listed in an expensive catalogue without incurring the cost of reprinting the catalogue. Usually the catalogue is issued with list prices that the wholesaler does not intend to enforce. The catalogue may be accompanied by a trade discount sheet which specifies that all the list prices are subject to a 20% trade discount or that certain categories of the goods may be subject to 20% and others subject to other rates. A few weeks later, in order to meet price competition or for other reasons, the wholesaler may distribute another discount sheet offering additional discounts on some or on all categories of goods. 

It must be understood that when several trade discounts are offered in a series (chain discounts) they are to be applied successively. In other words, an article listed at $1,000 and subject to

trade discounts of 20%, 10%, and 5% is intended to sell for $684 and not for $650. The easiest way to compute the price is to use the complements of the discount percentages (that is, $1,000 x .80 x .90 x .95 = $684 for the chain discounts of 20%, 10%, and 5%).

If a cash discount is offered in addition to a series of trade discounts, it must be calculated on the basis of the invoice price, that is, the list price less the trade discounts. If the item listed at $1,000 in the catalogue with applicable trade discounts of 20%, 10%, and 5% is subject to terms of 3/5, 2/10, n/30, it can be paid on the fifth day for .97 x $684; on the tenth day for .98 x $684; or on the thirtieth day for $684. Of course, even in this case, the cash discount terms might not be applied strictly. 

Some Illustrations of Current Entries 

The business practices discussed in this chapter are more clearly understood if actual illustrative entries are followed in the ledger, Figure 25. The transactions can be traced into the ledger by observing the key letters.

The beginning balances of the accounts are marked with asterisks (*). (a) Merchandise is purchased on account; the list price of $3,000 is subject to trade discounts of 30% and 5% and further subject to cash discount terms of 2/10, n/30. The invoice price is $1,995 (that is, $3,000 x .70 x .95). (b) The freight on the purchase (a) is paid in cash, $120. (c) One-third of the merchandise purchased in (a) is found to be unacceptable and is returned to the creditor. Note that the amount to be used in this transaction must be at the invoice price $665 (that is, $1,000 x .70 x .95). Note also that the buyer is in effect losing one-third of the amount paid for freight on the purchase. If the fault lies clearly with the vendor, the buyer might be able to charge $40 of the freight to the vendor. In the illustration, however, the full amount of $120 paid for freight is shown as a cost of the $1,330 purchase. (d) The amount due, after the previous transactions are completed, is paid within the ten-day cash discount period, $1,303.40 (that is, $1,330 x .98). (e) Sales of $200 subject to cash discount terms of 3/5, 2/10,

n/30 are made on account. Note that it is not necessary to know the cost of the goods involved in this sale since the entry is made by debiting Accounts Receivable and crediting Sales for the $200 sales price. (f) Of the goods sold, (e), $25 worth are damaged in transit, the damage being clearly traceable to improper crating by Lyman employees. The goods are not returned, but it is agreed that the customer may deduct the $25 from the amount owed. (g) On the fifth day after the goods are sold (e), the customer settles the amount due taking the 3% discount; he pays $169.75 net (that is, $175 x .97).

Income Statement and Closing Entries 

Having examined the flow of the current entries, we are now in a position to visualize the situation as it would appear at the end of an accounting period and to follow the steps necessary to close the merchandise accounts. Figure 26 shows the ledger with the balances of the accounts as they stand just prior to closing, indicated by asterisks (*), and with the entries to accomplish the closing indicated by key letters.

Generally the accountant wishes to give the result of the period's operations to management as promptly as possible. Therefore, he prepares the income statement as soon as the current entries have been made. The formal closing of the ledger at the end of the final period is illustrated in Figure 26 by the keyed entries, which can be made after the income statement has been prepared. 

The income statement in which this ledger information is gathered together for presentation to management is illustrated in Figure 27. Many minor differences in form would be possible and proper, but the statement illustrated in Figure 27 may be accepted as reasonably representative of income statements for small, singledepartment, trading businesses. In the older form, which is frequently found, the purchase discounts and sales discounts are shown as financial income and expense, respectively; these discounts are placed after the net operating income to produce a final figure called "net income," or "net income for the year 19XX." This alternate form is illustrated in Figure 28.

89

statement quite closely. Entries (a) and (b) close Sales Returns and Allowances and Sales Discounts accounts into the Sales account, giving the Sales account a balance which is the net sales amount for the period.

Entries (c), (d), and (e) close Freight-In, Purchase Returns and Allowances, and Purchase Discounts into the Purchases account. 

The addition of (c), and the subtraction of (d) and (e) leave a balance in the Purchases account which shows the amount of net purchases.

The four major trading facts can now be brought togetherthat is, the net sales, the net purchases, the beginning inventory, and the ending inventory. In the method illustrated, the Expense and Revenue account is used as the meeting place for these four items. Entries (f), (g), (h), and (i) bring the four items together in the Expense and Revenue account. Thus, the gross margin is, in effect, transferred to the Expense and Revenue account. All expenses have been closed into Expense and Revenue by one compound entry (j). This entry obviously saves time, since all expenses are listed and totaled in the income statement. Entry (k) closes the net income into the capital account. 

The closing entries in the ledger, as in the income statement, offer many opportunities for variations. Probably the most important alternative entry is a compound entry debiting Sales for the net sales amount, crediting Purchases for the net purchases amount, debiting or crediting Merchandise Inventory for the increase or decrease in the inventory, and crediting Expense and Revenue for the amount of the gross margin. For this entry the figures on the income statement are used as a guide. In addition to brevity, the compound entry has the advantage of making the figure for the gross margin stand out as the first figure on the credit side of the Expense and Revenue account. 

If the sales and purchase discounts are shown as financial expense and income in the income statement, they are closed into Expense and Revenue in the ledger to preserve the parallel with the statement.

Chapter II presented the problem of matching revenues and expenses in the income statement of a multiple-venture business. 

The ledger for such a business could be set up to parallel the sectional income statement. Essentially this arrangement requires a

separate Expense and Revenue account for each venture of the business in which the operating net profit for that venture can be developed. The net total of each of these special Expense and 

Revenue accounts is closed into a general Expense and Revenue account. This general account also is credited and debited for miscellaneous incomes and expenses which cannot sensibly be allocated to any one of the operating ventures. Many accountants, however, are satisfied to refine the analysis of the ventures only in the income statement and to close the ledger accounts on as simple a pattern as possible.

Another variation in the form of the income statement is especially useful as the business grows and becomes more complicated. In this type of statement, the main figures are shown in a summarized form and then are supported in separate detailed schedules. The income statement of Lyman Wholesale Drugs, rewritten in this form, is shown in Figure 29. Note particularly 

Schedules A, B, and C.

As the business grows larger, the figures for the sales and cost of sales may be separated by departments in the income statement. 

Similarly, the corresponding figures for some of the expenses may be separated into departmental expenses. Another development of the income statement may cause the expenses to be shown under major groupings, such as Purchasing Expenses, Selling Expenses,

General Administrative Expenses. The totals for each of these groups may be summarized in the income statement and supported by a separate schedule for each group. 

The summarized form of income statement, supported by detailed schedules, is advantageous for management since it presents without confusing detail the overall picture of the period's operations. Anyone interested in the details of any segment of the statement may then refer to the particular schedule in which he is interested.

In a very large business with transactions to be reported for dozens or hundreds of departments, divisions, sections, or other organization units, the summary form with supporting schedules is obviously the only technique which can adequately present the facts. The illustration, Figure 29, showing the comparatively simple Lyman Wholesale Drugs statement in summarized form is only the foretaste of a full development of this form.

Problems Based on Chapter VI 

1. Calculate the amount required to pay each of the following invoices at the highest cash discount offered. a. List price $10,000; trade discounts 20% and 10%; cash discount terms 3/5, 2/10, n/30. b. List price $80,000; trade discounts 15%, 72%; cash discount terms 2/10, n/30. c. List price $4,000; cash discount 2/10 E.O.M. n/60. (2/10 E.O.M. means 2% discount if paid within 10 days after the end of the month.) d. On an annual basis, calculate accurately to three decimal places the rate of interest which is involved in the cash discount terms in (a) above. 

2. a. Referring to the ledger and the income statement for the Lyman 

Wholesale Drug Company (Figures 26 and 27), calculate the corrected amounts for the gross margin and the net income which would result if an auditor should find that the December 31 inventory figure was overstated by $750. b. If the auditor in (a) above had discovered that the January 1 inventory had also been in error by an overstatement of $270, what should the gross margin and net operating income be? c. If the auditor also discovered that the $870 cost of a computing scale which was bought on December 29 for use in the delivery department had erroneously been charged to purchases, what would the corrected gross margin and net operating income be? d. If the auditor also discovered that supplies, $120, purchased on 

September 2 and entirely consumed by October 30 had been erroneously charged to purchases, what would the corrected gross margin and net operating income be? 

3. If someone in Lyman's employ had been careless during 19XX and had not taken a number of purchase discounts, the owner would certainly like to know it. Of the two methods of showing the purchase discounts in the income statements shown in the text, which would be more likely to reveal the employee's carelessness to the owner as he studied the statement? 

4. Dave Sellard's Haberdashery sells men's suits, overcoats, slacks, hats, socks, ties, belts, handkerchiefs, scarf pins, and so forth. Sellard works full time and has one full-time and two part-time clerks who sell anywhere from three or four to ten or fifteen of the larger items (suits and overcoats) per day and a much larger number of smaller

Chapter VI, Problem 4 (continued) 

items. Merchandise accounting has involved the taking of a physical inventory each month. Taking this inventory usually requires two and one-half man hours per month. 

Sellard is considering the conversion of his method of accounting to a profit-on-each-item method. This method would necessitate the marking of each item with its cost price (in code) as well as with its sales price, the noting of both sales price and cost price on a sales slip for each sale, the running of daily totals of sales and cost prices, and the entering of the daily totals in the ledger. The advantage, Sellard says, would be the avoidance of taking the monthly inventory, plus the fact that he would know his profit at the close of each day. 

Do you think he ought to switch to the new method? Analyze the situation fully, consider the costs and the advantages, and write a report stating your opinion. Give reasons for your views. 

5. a. The accompanying ledger of the Bergstrom Hardware Company represents the business status as of October 20 when you take charge as the bookkeeper. You are to make the remaining entries for 

October and to draw up the annual income statement and the statement of condition for October 31, the end of Bergstrom's fiscal year. 

You are also to close and rule the accounts. In this problem you are to respect discount terms rigidly. 

Transactions: 

Tuesday 

October 20 Purchases are made on account: List price $1,000 subject to a trade discount of 25% and terms 2/10, n/30.

Trucking charges on the purchases are paid in cash, $24. Wednesday 

October 21 Merchandise purchased yesterday, having a list price of $200, is returned to the vendor as unsatisfactory. Thursday 

October 22 Cash is collected on account, $1,250. Interest is received in cash, $20.

Friday October 23 Purchases are made on account, the invoice price is $1,600, terms are 3/5, n/30. Saturday 

October 24 The week's sales are recorded: On account, $1,100; for cash, $600. Of yesterday's purchases, merchandise returned as unsatisfactory totals, $200. Monday 

October 26 Cash, $15, is refunded on last week's cash sales. Cash is collected on account, $780.

CHAPTER VII  

THE REAL incurrence of expense is associated with the consumption of assets. This principle is not at all difficult to understand. 

However, the task of reflecting the actual expense factors in the accounts does present some practical difficulties, and this chapter is directed to a consideration of the means of overcoming these difficulties.

Incurrence of Expense 

Most expenses occur in a gradual manner, although not necessarily at a steady rate. Thus fuel for heating a store is being consumed continuously even though on cold days it is being consumed more rapidly than on mild days. Electricity for illumination likewise is being consumed throughout the twenty-four hours of the day in most establishments, although during closed hours the only light burning may be the one above the safe. Expenses, such as rent and insurance, are assumed to occur steadily throughout the month as a result of the passage of time. 

Wage expense in a retail store is generally thought of as accruing steadily on an hour-by-hour basis. If the clerks are on a commission basis, however, the commission expense, which takes the place of wages, occurs in an uneven stream as a result of the consummation of sales.

What can we say about the actual incurrence of advertising expense? Does this expense really occur when supplies are purchased for the advertising department? when the supplies are used in planning an advertising layout? when the newspaper bearing the advertisement is printed? when the newspaper is thrown on the front porch? when it is read in the evening? or when the sale is made the next day (or some days later) under the influence of the advertisement? Probably it is best to consider that the real incidence of advertising expense is determined by the resulting 101

sale because the attainment of sales is the real purpose of the whole advertising process.

As a matter of fact, careful analysis of the problem of the incidence of advertising expense gives a real clue to the whole problem of expense incidence. Fuel and electricity are measurable physical phenomena, and rent and insurance are clearly measurable in time. Thus each provides measurements by which the expense may be gauged. But advertising provides no such simple and obvious gauge; therefore, to determine the incidence of advertising expense we had to seek the essential factor. This essential factor turned out to be the benefit derived by the business from the advertisements.

Properly understood, this timing of the benefit to the business, is really the test we require to gauge the incidence of the fuel, electricity, wage, rent, and insurance expenses. In most cases, the simple tests of physical change or the passage of time are assumed to be proper measures of the incidence of expense because they are easy to apply and are close enough to the real benefit to serve for practical purposes.

It should be pointed out that the benefit which the business receives is usually the plus effect on its earning. This effect can be illustrated by noting the difference between the handling of expense items in a trading and in a manufacturing business. In a manufacturing business, fuel, wages, and insurance applicable to the manufacturing process are not considered expense at the time the fuel is being consumed, the labor is being performed, or the insurance is expiring. Instead, it is assumed that the manufacturing process results in adding values to the goods in the process of manufacture. The values thus added to the asset, completed product, are not considered an expense until the product is sold. This method of handling expenses quite clearly follows the benefit test for expense incidence because it is at the time of the sale that the profit is realized. By contrast, in the trading business, fuel, labor, and insurance are considered expense at the time of their original consumption or expiration.

Practical Expense Accounting 

From what has been said about expenses, it should be obvious that it would be impossible to make debit and credit techniques

portray the incurrence of expenses with full theoretic accuracy. 

It is certainly impracticable to measure and record expenses on a minute-by-minute basis as they occur. As a matter of fact, management has no use for such accuracy, since no practical management decisions depend on the expense figures for any particular minute.

Some expenses can be shown day by day on a basis which is reasonably accurate. The janitor can provide a daily report on the consumption of coal or oil, which can then be converted into dollars of fuel expense. Store clerks can be required to punch a card on a time clock to provide a record each day which can be entered into the Wage Expense account. The gas and electric meters can be read daily, and one thirtieth of the month's rent can be written up each day. In each case, however, the question must be asked, "Is such a degree of accuracy useful to management?" If the answer is not a clear “yes,” then it is an unwarranted waste of time and energy to produce records with such an unnecessary degree of accuracy.

Frequently it is desirable to handle one or more expense accounts in a system on an accurate basis, perhaps day-to-day, even though other accounts in the same system are not handled as accurately. It is not necessarily desirable to be consistent with respect to the degree of accuracy, especially since it is much more difficult to attain accuracy in some expenses than in others. 

The usual method of recording the expenses is to make an entry only when forced to make one by some overt action. For instance, most bookkeepers record fuel, rent, supplies, advertising, wage expense, and the like only when cash is being paid out or a specific item is purchased on account. This rather crude technique results in recognizing some items as expense before any benefit has been received and recognizing others after the benefit has been received. No harm is done, however, as long as the bookkeeper realizes that he is taking short cuts and as long as he makes the necessary correcting entries when accuracy of the expenses becomes significant, that is, at the end of the month or other period. 

Practically speaking, it is of no concern to management whether the expense accounts are up-to-date during the period, but management is very interested in having correct figures shown in the statements for the period. The bookkeeper, therefore, can use any short-cuts that he deems desirable during the period, but he must

go over all his resulting figures carefully at the end of the period before making up his statements. 

This task is referred to as "making adjustments." Some bookkeepers who do not quite understand the reasons for making adjustments naturally find this task rather fearsome, but when properly understood, the adjustments become just another piece of monthly routine.

Adjusting the Expense Accounts 

In order to adjust any expense account it is necessary to compare the entries that have been made with the known or ascertainable facts incident to the incurrence of the actual expense, and then to debit or to credit the account to increase or decrease the short-cut figures to make them tell the truth. For instance, if the bookkeeper has been debiting Fuel Expense every time that fuel was bought, the expense account is obviously overstated by the amount of fuel which remains on hand at the end of the period. 

Therefore, a credit entry must be made to Fuel Expense for the cost of the unconsumed fuel and a corresponding debit entry to an asset account, Coal on Hand, or to some other asset account with a similar title.

If the bookkeeper has been debiting Wage Expense when the store clerks are paid on each Saturday night and if the accounting period ends on Wednesday, the Wage Expense account must be debited for the amount of wages earned on Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday. A corresponding credit is made to an account called "Wages Accrued Payable" or to some other liability account with a similar title.

It should be noted that the adjustment of these two expense accounts required the recognition of an asset in one case and of a liability in the other. Since these amounts must be shown if the statement of condition is to be accurate, we must recognize now that the adjustments have a double significance, correcting the accounts to reflect the proper situations in the income statement and in the statement of condition. 

Adjusting the Revenue Accounts 

Everything that has been said about the expense accounts can be said with parallel significance about the revenue accounts. Rev-

enues occur with varying degrees of regularity. They do not have to be reflected in the accounts with minute accuracy. Here, too, the bookkeeper commonly makes entries only when faced by an overt happening, usually the receipt of cash. Thus, as in the case of the expense accounts, the revenue accounts do not even pretend to be accurate during the accounting period, but they must be scrutinized and adjusted at the end of the period to give them the accuracy which management requires. 

For instance, rent revenue of $100 per month actually may be received in advance of the month to which it pertains or it may be received at or after the end of the month. If the landlord is closing his books annually on December 31, it is of no concern to him that during the year, the rent revenue account shows only cash receipts of rent.

If the tenant pays $300 on January 10 for the months of January, February, and March, the bookkeeper debits Cash and credits Rent Revenue on January 10 when the cash is received. Since the $300 credit will not be entirely earned until the end of March, the Rent Revenue account overstates the revenue during the intervening period. If then the tenant pays no more during April and May, the bookkeeper makes no entries during those months, and the revenue shown in the Rent Revenue account is clearly understated. If the tenant pays $500 on June 4, and $300 on September 23, and if each receipt is credited to the Rent Revenue account, that account is seen to be always either over- or understated as compared to the true rent revenue earned. 

Presumably the landlord is not concerned whether the rent revenue is accurately reflected during the period, but on December 31, it would be necessary to examine the situation carefully and to make an entry to adjust the Rent Revenue account to the true rent earned. This adjustment would require a credit to the 

Rent Revenue account with a corresponding debit to "Rent Accrued Receivable," "Rent Revenue Accrued," or some such title showing clearly that the $100 asset was receivable. 

If on the other hand the tenant had paid another $300 on December 12, the Rent Revenue account would have been overstated by two months' revenue on December 31. The adjustment for this situation would require a debit to Rent Revenue and a credit to "Rent Revenue Received in Advance," "Deferred Rent

Revenue," or some similarly descriptive title. Such an account would be a liability and would be shown as such in the statement of condition.

The ledger of the Fleming Creek Trading Company is shown in Figure 30. The account balances marked with asterisks are the balances as of December 31 after all current entries for the year have been made but before adjusting and closing entries have been made. The following data were taken into account by the bookkeeper in adjusting the expense and revenue accounts preparatory to closing. The adjusting entries may be traced by the key letters. (a) Wages accrued since the last payday are $180. (b) Fuel left on hand is estimated at $75. (c) Detailed records of tenants' payments show that rent for January and February, amounting to $850, has been received in advance from various tenants. (d) An examination of the face amounts, interest rates, and payment dates of bonds owned indicates that interest amounting to $120 has been earned but has not yet been received.

In Figure 30, closing entries are made without key letters, but they can be traced by their identical amounts. Note especially that it is always the adjusted balance which is closed into the Expense and Revenue account whenever any adjustment has been made.

This illustration contains one example of each of four technical adjustments. Notice that the expenses were affected in two ways (wages increased, fuel decreased) and that revenues were affected in two ways (rent decreased and interest increased). Notice also that out of the adjusting process arose two new assets and two new liabilities. The recognition of this "pattern" in the adjustment situation will aid you in the practical task of making the adjustments. If you keep the general pattern of the possible adjustments in mind you will be able to recognize the need for making each individual adjustment as it is presented. 

If the Fleming Creek Trading Company had been an actual firm, very likely additional adjustments would have been necessary. For instance, it is quite likely that close examination would disclose substantial amounts of office supplies on hand, as well as

unexpired insurance premiums. The adjustments for these two items would be similar to the adjustment for fuel. Both adjustments would, require decreases in the expense accounts and the setting up of new accounts to recognize the assets involved (Office 

Supplies on Hand, and Prepaid Insurance). The Light Expense and Telephone Expense quite likely would require adjustments similar to the adjustment for wage expense, since charges for electricity and telephone service usually are billed several weeks after the service has been consumed. 

Since there are so few revenues, there are relatively few opportunities for parallel adjustments. It is quite possible, however, to have rent accrued receivable from some tenants while other tenants have paid in advance. We might, therefore, be required to make another adjustment in the Rent Revenue account similar to the $120 interest adjustment. If some tenants have paid in advance while others owe for past periods, adjustments must be made to reflect both facts rather than to "net out" the two amounts.

In the Interest Revenue account we are often forced to make an entry similar to that for the rent received in advance.¹ 

The Adjustments and the Statement of Condition 

The two kinds of asset accounts (Accrued Incomes and Deferred Expenses) and the two kinds of liability accounts (Accrued Expenses and Deferred Revenues) must of course be shown in the statement of condition as assets and liabilities. The accountant, however, need not list these accounts in full detail, but may combine similar items under group titles. Thus the separate accrued incomes may be shown on the statement of condition as one item, "Accrued Income," and the several accrued expenses may be shown as one item, "Accrued Expense." The several deferred expenses become "Deferred Expense," and the deferred incomes become "Deferred Income."

A similar concentration may be used in the ledger, if desired, by using accounts with the four group titles and by labeling each 'Some authorities contend that "interest received in advance" is a misnomer. They contend that loans made on a discount basis are basically no different from loans made on interest-bearing notes and that either the discounted value of the instrument should be shown as the asset or the face amount should be shown in an asset account with the amount of the discount shown as a contra or subtraction from the face amount.

specific item at the side. Any such grouping, either in the ledger or in the statement of condition, should not be allowed, however, to obscure the facts concerning any single large or important items which management might wish to watch closely. Such items should be set up separately in order to disclose them as fully as management desires. 

Varying the "Adjustment Pattern"

In the illustrations we have assumed that such items as fuel and insurance are debited to expense accounts when purchased and must, therefore, be adjusted as shown. Many bookkeepers, however, prefer to debit these and similar items to asset accounts at the time of purchase and then later to record the fact of consumption by debiting expense and crediting the asset. These entries can be made on a more or less current basis as consumption is reported, or they can be made at the end of the accounting period, when they can be handled as an "adjustment." In a large organization such an item as office supplies may be handled in this way. 

When supplies are purchased, they are debited to an account named "Office Supplies," or "Office Supplies in Stockroom." The actual supplies are then kept by a custodian under lock and key and are issued for use only on written requisition by authorized department heads. When these requisitions are priced and summarized, they become evidence and are the occasion for entries debiting expense and crediting the asset account. This method is used when the volume of business is large enough to make it feasible, since it indicates the true incidence of the expense much more closely than does the short-cut method of debiting expense at the time of the purchase. The additional cost of this method is amply justified by the need to protect the supplies from pilferage or waste. An incidental advantage that results from better custodianship is more accurate accounting. 

When these more formal methods of accounting for expense are in use, the adjustments at the end of the period assume the aspect of correcting errors in the routine process rather than rectifying deviations caused by previous short cuts. For instance, in the routine described for office supplies, it might be desirable to take an actual inventory of the supplies in the stockroom at the end of the period in order to find any errors that may have crept into

the routine entries. If the Office Supplies in Stockroom account shows a balance of $4,000, but the actual inventory shows only $3,850, it may be assumed that some of the requisitions did not reach the bookkeeper or have been filled in error. The adjustment would require a debit to Office Expense and a credit to the asset account for the $150 error. The opposite situation, inventory in excess of Stockroom account, would call for an adjusting entry the reverse of this one. If there is evidence to indicate that the shortage in the inventory of office supplies was not caused by an honest error but by pilferage, it would be improper to debit Office Expense. In this case the debit should be to a very special expense account, "Loss by Theft of Office Supplies," or to a sim ilarly descriptive title. 

A common variation from the short-cut pattern for adjusting expense items is found in making the entries for wages. When employees punch in and out daily on a time clock, it is customary to make an entry each day debiting Wage Expense and crediting "Wages Accrued Payable” or “Accrued Payroll." The entry for actual payment then becomes a debit to one of these accounts and a credit to Cash. If this system is operating properly, there is no need for any adjustment at the end of the accounting period, because the expense account is brought up-to-date daily by the routine entries.

In actual practice the bookkeeper may use a mixture of these methods. The only possible way to safeguard end-of-the-period correctness is to examine the pattern of the relationship between the actual items of expense and income and the bookkeeping entries and then to adjust every item on its merits. 

Special Adjustments for Asset Accounts 

In addition to the adjustments caused by typical short-cuts in the recording of revenues and expenses, it is usually necessary at the end of the accounting period to recognize certain decreases in asset values, which cannot very well be recognized on a day-today basis. Major items of this kind are the depreciation of such assets as buildings, furniture, trucks, and machinery. 

Basically, the purchase and consumption of these long-lived assets are no different from the purchase and consumption of short-lived assets, such as coal or office supplies. The difference is

one of degree and not of kind. The degree of difference is sufficient, however, to merit quite different handling in the accounts. 

As pointed out in an earlier chapter, the difference in their relative permanence is recognized by the use of the terms "fixed assets" and "current assets" with the dividing line between them at the traditional one-year period, although this period is by no means a rigid limit. 

Fixed assets are always debited at the time of purchase under descriptive account titles. The inevitable decrease in value of fixed assets must be spread equitably over the years in which the benefit is obtained from the use of the asset. Depreciation expense is the debit which results when the cost of any fixed asset is apportioned among the incomes of the accounting periods concerned. 

Note that it is not possible to gauge the decrease in value of a building or a truck by making an examination of the kind that can be made for such assets as coal or office supplies. Much has been said and written about the nature of the curve along which fixed asset values are assumed to depreciate. The method of depreciating fixed assets which is most widely accepted is the straight line method. Under this method, equal portions of the original cost are charged to expense in each accounting period during which the asset is used.

In very simple circumstances, the original cost of the asset is divided by the number of accounting periods (years, quarters, or months) of useful life expected for the asset. If it is assumed that the asset will still have some value at the time of its disposal (scrap or residual value), the periodic depreciation is the cost minus this residual value divided by the number of periods. Of course both the residual value and the expected period of useful life are at best estimates, and sometimes the original estimates are far from accurate. The accountant does the best he can with the information and advice available; when previous estimates have been proved wrong, he changes his depreciation accounting to correspond with the newer, better estimates. 

The entry to be made for the periodic depreciation on a building is a debit to the Depreciation Expense account and a credit to Buildings. A variant of this basic entry is used in most well developed accounting systems. It requires a decrease in value of the building to be credited to a new account "Allowance for De-

preciation of Building" instead of to the Building account itself, but this entry does not change the real significance of the credit. 

This new account was formerly called “Reserve for Depreciation," but the word reserve has acquired so many different meanings that it causes confusion. The use of the word “allowance" avoids this confusion.

The Allowance for Depreciation account should always be shown in the ledger right next to the account showing the asset which it helps to evaluate. In the statement of condition we may show either the net value of the asset or the cost in the short position with the amount of the allowance subtracted and the net extended; the latter practice is preferred as it gives a more complete picture, unless there is a serious limitation of space. A building with a net value of $40,000 might be either a new building which cost $45,000 and has depreciated $5,000 to date or an old building which cost $120,000 and is two-thirds depreciated. The difference between these two situations is significant to anyone in management who has occasion to use the statement of condition The crediting of decreases to an Allowance account, instead of directly to the asset, keeps these relationships more clearly in view.

Although the Depreciation Expense account is closed at the end of each year to the Expense and Revenue account, the Allowance for Depreciation account is not closed until the asset to which it pertains has been disposed of. Note that the allowance account is really nothing more than the credit side of an asset account. The life history of an asset is illustrated in Figure 31. 

In the illustration it appears that machine number 62 was purchased for $12,500 cash on January 1, 1947, the entry being a debit to Machine #62 and a credit to Cash. At that time the life of the machine was estimated to be ten years and the probable scrap value was estimated to be $500. Thus the total depreciable value is $12,000, and this figure divided by ten (the estimated life of the machine) gives $1,200 as the amount of the annual depreciation. At the end of each year the accountant's adjustment entry is a debit to Depreciation Expense and a credit to Allowance for

Depreciation of Machine #62. Each December 31 the Deprecia tion Expense account is closed to Expense and Revenue, along with all other expenses. The Allowance account, however, remains open

and shows the accumulation of the depreciation from year to year. 

At the end of the third year, for instance, the Allowance for Depreciation of Machine # 62 shows a credit balance of $3,600. 

The statement of condition at that date, December 31, 1949, would show in its fixed asset section: 

Machine #62 

Less: Allowance for Depreciation .... $12,500 

3,600 $8,900

The figure of $8,900 is known as the book value of the machine. 

This figure does not pretend to show “value” in the sense of what the machine can be sold for, what its replacement cost is, nor how important it is to the owner as part of his going business. The figure shows only that portion of the cost of the machine which has not yet been charged off to depreciation expense. 

At the end of the tenth year, December 31, 1956, the statement of condition would show: 

Machine #62 

Less: Allowance for Depreciation... $12,500 

12,000 $500

At this time the book value has been reduced to the expected scrap or residual value of the machine.

The illustration assumes that the estimated ten-year life of the machine is proven correct and that the machine is retired from operation and sold for $475 on January 1, 1957. Since the amount obtained is $25 less than the amount expected, it is necessary to recognize this $25 as a loss on retirement of the machine. 

It should be noted that at this time, January 1, 1957, the accounts (Machine #62 and Allowance for Depreciation of Machine #62) are both closed out. The entry to close these accounts and to recognize the sale of the machine is a compound entry, consisting of a debit to cash, $475; a debit to Allowance for Depreciation of Machine #62, $12,000; a debit to Loss on Retirement, $25; and a credit to Machine #62, $12,500.

If more than the expected amount is received when the discarded machine is sold for scrap, a credit results which is called 

Profit on Retirement of Machine. If both profits and losses may be expected, the accountant may set up an account called Profit and Loss on Retirement of Equipment to take entries of both kinds.

Losses on retirement of equipment result quite commonly when assets are discarded before the expected term of life has run. 

Thus, if Machine #62, Figure 31, had been discarded on January 1, 1955, there would have been only $9,600 in the allowance account, leaving a book value of $2,900. Even if the sale of the machine for scrap brought the amount originally estimated, $500, there would still be a loss on retirement of $2,400. 

This problem and related problems are more completely treated in Accounting II. 

In Figure 32, the ledger of P. Caulfield, Commission Merchant, shows account balances (marked with asterisks) as of the end of 

Caulfield's fiscal year, , before adjusting entries are made. The adjustment data to be recorded can be traced into the ledger by the following key letters: (a) Wages accrued, $300 (b) Utility bills accrued, $62 (c) Advertising materials on hand, $140 (d) Insurance unexpired, $1,200 (e) Rent revenue collected but not yet earned, $90 (f) Interest received but not yet earned, $20 (g) Rent accrued receivable, $240 (h) Interest earned on bonds but not yet collected, $80 (i) Estimated depreciation on the building, $1,000 (j) Estimated depreciation on the furniture and fixtures, $1,800 (k) Estimated depreciation on the delivery truck, $800. 

The closing entries are not keyed but can be traced by the identity of the amounts. Note that in every case it is the adjusted amount of expense and revenue which is closed to the Expense and Revenue account. The statement of condition as of the close of the year is given in Figure 33. Note especially the manner in which the deferred and accrued expenses and incomes are shown, and the manner in which the allowances for depreciation are deducted from the related asset accounts. 

Disposition of Deferred and Accrued Items The disposition of the deferred and accrued expenses and incomes and their effect upon the net income of the subsequent accounting period must be thought out carefully.

The two deferred items are the easiest to understand. In the illustration, Figure 33, the two deferred expense items were advertising materials and unexpired insurance. Since these items will be consumed in the subsequent period, they ought to be shown as expenses in that period. One way to reflect this condition in the accounts is to make an entry on July 1, the first day of the new period, transferring these amounts into the expense accounts. The entries required would be: debit Advertising Expense $140, debit Insurance Expense $1,200, and credit Deferred

Expense $1,340. Since the two deferred incomes (rent $90 and interest $20) will be earned and thus become income in the new period, the accounts should show this fact. The entries required would be: debit Deferred Income, $110, credit Rent Revenue $90, and credit Interest Income $20. These entries are illustrated in the second Caulfield ledger shown in Figure 34.

The accrued incomes and accrued expenses are assets and liabilities closely comparable to accounts receivable and accounts payable. The balances set up as the result of the adjustments may be allowed to "ride” in the Accrued Income and Accrued Expense accounts until they are collected or paid. At time of collection or payment, they may be handled as an account receivable or an account payable. Since the collection and payment of these items
