121

is closely associated with the incomes and expenses of the subsequent period, however, this method tends to be confusing when the transactions are actually mingled with the welter of current entries two or three weeks after the beginning of the new period. 

The more practical way to solve this difficulty is to make an entry at the opening of the new accounting period to transfer these items into the accounts in which they originated, although, of course, in the new section of those accounts below the rulings for the old closings. After the transferring entries have been made, the actual collection or payment involved can be handled as though it were current income or expense in the new period. The resulting balance in the affected income and expense accounts will measure correctly the income and expense of the new period. 

An actual illustration will aid in clarifying these entries; in 

Figure 34 the pertinent parts of Caulfield's ledger are shown after the retransfer entries have been made on July 1, the first day of the new fiscal year. These entries have been keyed with the symbol (J1). It should be noted that in the expense and revenue accounts the July 1 figure appears below the ruling on the opposite side of the account from which it appeared above the ruling. This observation can be used as a mechanical check against errors in making the entries.

Figures 35 and 36 show in graphic form the technical adjustments for the end-of-the-period and the retransfer entries for the beginning-of-the-period. These charts should be studied carefully and compared with the presentations of the debit and credit figures in the Caulfield ledgers, Figures 33 and 34. It is especially important to note exactly where (whether above or below the closing rulings) the arrows originate and terminate. It will be remembered that the figures above the rulings in the expense and income accounts pertain to the end-of-the-period work, and that the figures below the rulings pertain to the work of the new period just beginning.

A final variant in the handling of the technical adjustments can be regarded as growing out of the full method of the endof-period and the retransfer entries as previously explained. By this method the bookkeeper simply neglects to make the beginning-of-the-period retransfer entries. The balances in the deferred and accrued income and expense accounts are allowed to remain

in these accounts throughout the accounting period. Then at the end of the ensuing period when the problem of adjustments again arises, the old balances of accrued and deferred items must be taken into consideration. At this time a special adjustment is made for the difference between the old and the new accrual or deferral. This method is illustrated in Figure 37. 

In Figure 37, notice first the balances under the date January 1, 1955 in the four accounts for accrued and deferred income and expenses: interest, coal, wages, and rent. Notice that these balances were not retransferred to the expense and income accounts at the beginning of the year. Notice that the Fuel Expense account and the Wage Expense account received a stream of debits during the year representing fuel purchases and wage payments. Notice that the Interest Income account and the Rent Income account received credits at various dates representing the receipt of interest or rent.

Notice that on December 31, 1955 four adjustments were made, each of which brought one of the deferred or accrued items into agreement with the actual status of the facts as of the end of the year. The interest accrued receivable on December 31 was $92; therefore, an addition of $17 to the old balance of Interest Income Accrued was required to reflect the present fact. The corresponding credit of $17 to the Interest Income account brings its balance up to the true total of interest earned for the year. 

Since there is only $270 of coal on hand at the year's end, an amount of $80 must be subtracted from the old balance of $350 to reflect the current condition. The corresponding debit to Fuel 

Expense makes that account indicate the consumption of $2,080 of fuel for the year.

Wages Accrued Payable is increased by $115 to bring it up to the $390 point; the debit being to Wage Expense, to make that account show the true amount of $42,705 of wage service consumed for the year.

Rent Received in Advance was debited $120 to bring it to the proper balance, $360, for December 31, the Rent Income account receiving the $120 credit to bring it to the true $6,135 of rent earned for the year.

The corrected balances of the two income accounts and the two expense accounts were closed to Expense and Revenue. These

figures are used in the income statement. The debit balances of $92 and $270 in the Interest Income Accrued and Coal on Hand accounts are shown as assets in the statement of condition. The credit balances of $390 and $360 in the Wages Accrued Payable and Rent Received in Advance accounts are shown as liabilities. 

In practical bookkeeping the various techniques discussed in this chapter are often used in combination. A new bookkeeper or a public accountant hired to audit the books cannot safely assume that any one method is used systematically. He must be be prepared to look at every income and every expense with a critical eye and to judge whether it reflects the facts of the case. 

Actually this decision is not a difficult one if he will keep in mind the basic concepts of what constitutes an asset, a liability, an income, or an expense. The problem of adjustments can be summed up by stating that we are trying to make each account tell the truth.

Chapter VII, Problem 5 

5. The Red Coach Trailer Court, whose ledger as of December 31, 19XX is shown herewith, rents space with utility connections, sells trailers, and runs a small store on the grounds.

Trailers are usually sold with a 25 per cent cash down payment and a 12or 18-month instalment note for the balance. At the time of each sale Cash and Notes Receivable are debited and Trailer Sales is credited. The cost of the trailer is immediately debited to 

Trailer Cost of Sales and credited to Trailers Owned. Rental con tracts for space are on a weekly basis. Each week the bookkeeper debits Space Rents Receivable and credits Space Rentals (the income account). Collections of space rentals are debited to Cash and credited to Space Rents Receivable. A detailed record (not shown in this problem) is kept for every tenant, and can be reconciled at any time with the control account, Space Rents Receivable. 

Merchandise sales, purchases, and returns are handled in the normal retail manner. 

This problem requires performing the following tasks: a. To make a few routine entries b. To adjust the accounts preparatory to closing c. To draw up the year's income statement and the December 31 statement of condition d. To close and rule the books 

The following information must be considered.

Under requirement a, December 31 transactions were: Merchandise sales, cash $50, on account $80. Chapter VII, Problem 6 

6. A customer of your bank, who will probably ask the bank to help him in financing the purchase, is considering the purchase of the Red Coach Trailer Court. The price which your client will be willing to pay will depend on the probable net income which he will be able to make from the business; hence your report should consider any feasible changes which would obviously affect the income after the transfer. 

Your inquiry into the reason for the high margin in the merchandise sales (over 43%) brings out the fact that the bookkeeper has included as sales the agent's commission which the store receives for acting as a branch for the City Laundry and Dry Cleaners. From detailed records you ascertain that these commissions totaled $16,527 in 19XX. After this correction the gross margin for the store is still quite high for groceries. Appliance sales were $12,000 during the past year.

Part II MANAGEMENT AND THE LARGE-VOLUME PROBLEM CHAPTER VIII  IN THE FIRST Seven chapters of this book the basic business and accounting relationships are discussed. In one sense these chapters present the story of accounting completely. Within this framework, every possible type of transaction that can happen in any business can be recognized and recorded in accounting terms for management's use.

This presentation is theoretically complete; however, it becomes of much greater value when it is extended into and studied in the light of the realistic, large-volume conditions of actual business. The difference between twenty-five transactions and 

25,000 transactions is in theory merely a multiplication factor; in actual practice there is so much detail involved in handling a large volume of transactions that this difference in degree results, in effect, in an independent realm of study. The student should keep in mind the real nature of the problem faced in setting up a mature accounting system. The problem is to handle realistic volumes of transactions in a practical, economical way. It is not, however, simply the provision of bigger baskets for larger volumes of undifferentiated facts. In order to classify and summarize these facts properly, we must keep in mind constantly the purposes for which they are being collected in the useful categories that have already been developed in our basic studies. 

In studying accounting systems it is useful to recognize the fact that the work of the accountant falls into four phases or functions. In earlier chapters, the ledger function and the statement and reports function have been presented, but chronologically these 143

are the third and fourth functions. Preceding these functions in the full accounting system are two functions, immediately involved with the practical matter of large volume, the original record function and the journal function. 

The Original Record Function 

The first function is the original record function. In its early evolution it was represented by an actual book called the daybook. 

This daybook was simply a sturdily bound blank book in which the proprietor and his employees wrote down in colloquial form whatever happened in the business which was of obvious dollar and cents importance. "Sold 4 bbls. pork at $4.50 per bbl. on account to Hiram Todd"; "Purchased for cash, new steelyard at $1.35 for use in poultry dept."; "Lightning set fire to smoke-house causing total loss of $110.00," and so forth.

It is easy to visualize the proprietor exhorting his employees, "Now don't trust your memory for any dollars and cents matters, write them down immediately in the daybook!"

Today it would probably be impossible to find a daybook as such in any business of importance, but the function originally performed by the daybook is still exactly the same as it was one hundred years ago. The old bound daybook, however, has been blown up into a hundred different forms, papers, devices, and machines, all of them aimed at the same fundamental purpose, namely, to get a true and ineradicable record of what has happened. Invoices, check stubs, requisitions, time tickets, receipts, time clock punches, cash register punches, and the like in tremendous variety-these are the modern daybook record, and together they comprise the original record function. The designing of these business papers and the setting up of the necessary rules and regulations for their proper use is itself a highly technical matter. Some of the records are produced in multiple copies, occasionally as many as eight, ten, or more. It is a commonplace criticism to scoff at "all these unnecessary extra copies," but the real office technician knows that frequently the creation of multiple copies is the easiest and most economical method of getting the necessary work accomplished.

Among the multiple copies of any document there is usually one that can be identified as "the accounting copy." This copy

“carries the ball" as far as subsequent accounting records and their use by management are concerned. It is with this copy that we are primarily concerned in the text. In fact in many discussions and often in formal descriptions or flow charts of accounting systems, only this one copy of the original record will even be mentioned, but the accountant knows and appreciates the uses that are made of the other copies in other aspects of the office routine. 

THE JOURNALS 

After its recognition in the daybook stage, any bit of information may be followed as it enters the journal phase or function. 

In any practical bookkeeping system, the journal as a written record may take an apparently endless variety of forms, but one essential feature is always found. It is the analysis of the factual data into its debit and credit effects. The performance of the journal function requires a knowledge of the basic accounting relationships which enable the bookkeeper-accountant to decide which accounts are involved and how the pluses and minuses fall. 

Actually the student has already been performing the journal function every time he made an entry in the ledger. The pure journal function is the mental operation of analysis. In practical bookkeeping this journalizing function is made a matter of specific record each time it is performed. The pure journal function is most obvious in the simple journal. As the journal develops, it takes on certain other facilitative aspects until often its original function seems overshadowed by its other uses. Practically, then, we may now think of the journal function as the problem of marshaling detailed entries at the point of their first debit and credit recognition with as much attention given to their economical accumulation as to their basic relationships. 

The Simple Journal 

The simple journal page is ruled with two amount columns at the right. It may have other rulings to indicate the placing of dates, equal indentations and so forth, but the two amount columns are its essential characteristics. In this journal each transaction is entered or journalized as a unit. Always the name of the account to be debited is written first, beginning at the left margin.

The name of the account to be credited is written indented on the next line. If more than one account is to be debited, all the titles are written in the debit position on successive lines followed by the titles of all the accounts to be credited. The amounts are placed in the amount columns on the same line as the related titles, using the left and right columns for the debits and credits respectively. An examination of the accompanying simple journal entries, Figure 38, should make this discussion clearer. 

The illustration of the simple journal includes sample entries of current transactions, adjusting entries, and closing entries. Note that explanations are given with certain entries to avoid danger of confusion or to preserve incidental information. Such explanations should contain all the information necessary to a complete understanding of the entry. If the nature of the transaction is perfectly clear from the titles of the accounts debited and credited, it is plainly wasteful to write explanations. 

The small column headed "F" (for folio, or page) is used to indicate the transferring, technically known as “posting,” of the items to their accounts in the ledger. The numbers in the folio column, Figure 38, indicate that the bookkeeper has posted all the current items for December 30 and 31, the numbers also indicate the pages on which the items were posted. He has not yet posted the adjusting or closing entries made on December 31. In practical bookkeeping the form used for ledger accounts provides for a reference to the source of each item posted, that is, to the page on which the item appears in the journal. Thus there is always a complete tracing system for anyone who might be interested in checking the entries in-and-out at any later date. 

In simple business situations, such a journal and a ledger often constitute the entire bookkeeping system, but in more complicated businesses, it would be wise to use a more highly developed form of journal, such as those described in later sections of this chapter and the next.

The Multicolumnar Journal 

The posting of many entries from the simple journal would be tedious work, as well as a prolific source of error. The multicolumnar journal is the obvious answer to the posting difficulty. 

This new form provides special columns for the types of entries

which repeat frequently. The repetitions may be cumulated in the journal and posted in total, instead of being posted in detail. 

Transactions that occur frequently are cash receipts and cash disbursements. Therefore, a pair of columns must be provided for cash, one for the debits and one for the credits; which other types of transactions merit special columns depends on the kind of business. Sales credits and purchase debits are frequent in a trading business. Rent revenue is frequent in a housing project, interest revenue in a bank, and various expenses occur frequently in any business. Debits and credits to accounts receivable and accounts payable occur frequently in many businesses, but the use of these columns is discussed in a later chapter in connection with customers' and creditors' subsidiary ledgers. 

A few sample entries in a multicolumnar journal are shown in Figure 39. The column headings and the nature of the transactions indicate that these are the books of a private capitalist. It should be noted that the transactions assumed to be frequent enough to merit special column treatment are cash debits and credits and interest and dividend income credits. Notice that entries to specific investment accounts, Profit on Sale, Loss on Sale, and to specific assets and specific expenses must still be entered in the general debit and credit columns. 

Notice that in Figure 39, a debit and a credit are entered on the same line in most cases. This method conserves vertical space without sacrificing clarity since most of the items are entered in the specialized columns. Since the posting references are shown in the folio column for each figure that must be posted individually, the economy of space is desirable. It would never be desirable to sacrifice clarity for economy of space.

John Dough's journal is accompanied by his ledger, Figure 40, which is set up in formal style with space provided for dates, explanations, and posting references for each figure posted into the accounts. Since this formal style of ruling does not have the advantage of grouping the accounts together as they are shown in the previous single page ledgers, the accounts are numbered systematically in a manner similar to the numbering schemes commonly used in practice. All asset accounts are numbered in the one hundreds, the liabilities in the two hundreds, the capital accounts in the three hundreds, the income accounts in the four

hundreds, and the expense accounts in the five hundreds. This scheme would not be necessary in a simple situation, but in businesses having a large number of accounts, systematic numbering is a very great aid.

The principal advantage of the multicolumnar journal is demonstrated in posting the entries to the cash account which shows one debit and one credit for the month, although the cash transactions were running at a rate that would indicate a hundred or more entries per month. The single credits to accounts #401 and #402 also demonstrate the posting economy provided by this form of journal. It is customary to indicate the posting of the totals by showing the number of the account below the total in the columns of the journal.

Observe the initialed notation on the total line. It is obviously good judgment to test the debit and credit equality of the totals before posting them in order to find any errors of entry or addition. As a matter of fact, in order to localize errors, this test of debit and credit equality should be made at the bottom of each page, rather than at the end of the month. Not all errors are found, however, even with this degree of care. If a figure denoting dividend income is erroneously placed in the interest income column, it is obvious that the debit and credit equality is not disturbed.

Attention is invited to the ruling and bringing down of totals in two of the ledger accounts, #101 and #113, which are not involved in the adjusting or closing processes. Note that the ruling for the addition is drawn only across the amount columns, whereas the double rulings for "balance" are drawn across the amount, date, and folio columns. This pattern may be accepted as one of the conventions of bookkeeping, although this particular styling has no great fundamental significance. When accounts happen to balance with a single entry on each side, as in the case of Smith Steel Company, there is no need to show single rules and totals. 

The double rules indicate very clearly that the account has been closed.

It should be noted that there is little or no need for the special columns for adjusting and closing entries. These entries can be placed in the two general columns as though they were placed in a simple two-column journal.

Accounts Receivable and Control Accounts 

Thus far a practical fact has been disregarded: In dealing with customers the accountant must provide for a detailed record of each transaction with every customer as well as for the total information regarding accounts receivable contained in the ledger and in the statement of condition. The performance of this necessary task requires the establishment of a new ledger for individual customers' accounts. This ledger must show, in full detail, the same information which appears totaled in the Accounts Receivable account. Such a relationship is known as a control account and subsidiary ledger relationship. 

It would be very confusing if the bookkeeper had to keep parallel information flowing to the control and subsidiary accounts while making entries directly into the ledger accounts, as we do in earlier chapters, or even while making entries through a simple journal. The task is very easy, however, when the special column device is used.

A pair of special columns is set up in the journal for debits and credits involving the control account, Accounts Receivable. The totals of these columns are posted to the control account at the end of each month, and the detailed items in the columns are posted to the individual customers' accounts in the customers' ledger. This detailed posting should be done currently to provide an up-to-date balance for each customer's account at all times. 

At the end of each month the total of the balances of all the customers' accounts should agree with the balance of the Accounts Receivable control account in the general ledger. Considering the very large volume of transactions a business has with its customers, such checking is a desirable guarantee of accuracy. 

The form of the individual customer's account varies considerably. For the problems in this text, the customers' ledger may be a sheet of paper with T-accounts. In small businesses it may be a bound book having the standard ledger ruling; a page or two may be allowed for each customer with all postings entered manually. Most larger businesses use machine posting; the customers' ledger is a tray or a file box (or a cabinet full of trays or file boxes) in which each customer's account is a loose card or a sheet of heavy manila paper suitable for repeated insertion in the bookkeeping

machine. The machined form is usually a debit, credit, and balance form, similar in appearance to the machined form for the Investments account that is shown earlier in Figure 16 of Chap ter IV. 

Other Control Accounts and Subsidiary Ledger Relationships 

Everything that has been said about problems of customer accounts could be said about the problems of creditor accounts. 

Most firms have fewer creditors than customers, but the number of creditors and the volume of transactions is still large enough to justify a control account in the general ledger supported by a subsidiary ledger which gives the full details about each creditor account.

The journal has a pair of special columns for all transactions with creditors. All credits for purchases on account are entered in the credit column. The debits for cash payments, notes given on account, and purchase returns and allowances are entered in the debit column. All detailed items in these columns are posted currently and the totals are posted at the end of the month. The balances of the individual creditors' accounts are totaled at the end of the month to verify their agreement with the Accounts 

Payable control account.

In addition to the accounts receivable and accounts payable situations, the control account and subsidiary ledger relationship is often found in several other situations. In a corporation the capital stock account is a control account and must be supported by detailed records showing the exact amount of stock owned by each stockholder. If there are only a few stockholders, the detailed record may comprise nothing more than the stubs of the stock certificate book. With hundreds of stockholders-especially if the stock is dealt in actively-the detailed record of stockholders may be a standard ruled ledger with an account for each stockholder. 

The record would probably be kept in terms of the number of shares owned and purchased or sold by each stockholder rather than in terms of dollars. In very large corporations whose stock is actively traded on the exchanges, the task of keeping the stock ledger is contracted to banks or trust companies, which act as transfer agents and specialize in this function. In any of these cases it is correct to regard the Capital Stock account as a control

account. In this case the balance of the control account may remain the same for extended periods of time-until additional shares are sold by the corporation or until some shares are redeemed. At all times the total number of shares recorded as owned by all the individual stockholders must agree with the number of shares outstanding as shown by the Capital Stock control account.

Another control account-subsidiary ledger relationship is found in the accounts of manufacturing companies whose expense accounts are both very numerous and very active. The general ledger may show an account called "Expense Control," with the total therein detailed in a subsidiary ledger containing hundreds or thousands of separate expense accounts. Sometimes the general ledger of a manufacturing concern shows the control account broken into three control accounts, one for each major division of the business, as follows: Selling Division Expense Control, General Administrative Division Expense Control, and Manufactur ing Division Expense Control. These accounts are supported by the detailed accounts in three sections of the expense ledger, each of which must be reconciled with its own control account. 

It is important that the student grasp the basic concept of control accounts for the typical accounting situations previously presented in order to be able to apply the principles to similar situations which he may encounter in business. Obviously the special columns in the multicolumnar journal lend themselves very well to the task of keeping the accounts and the ledgers in agreement. Special columns in the special journals (presented in the next chapter) perform the same function, and still other techniques related to the use of bookkeeping machines aid in this task.

CHAPTER IX  IN CHAPTER VIII the four basic phases or functions of account ing were outlined: the original record function, the journal function, the ledger function, and the reports and statements function. 

Each of these records makes its own unique contribution to the overall task of accounting. Superimposed on these four basic functions is the general function of summarization. 

THE FUNCTION OF SUMMARIZATION 

Management, in a business of any size, seldom consults the detailed record of transactions. In their original states these records are a conglomerate mass that is practically meaningless; it is only after a series of operations, in which they are sorted, recapitulated, and totaled, that they become useful to management. This process can be thought of as the summarization function. It is very important to understand and to visualize correctly the relationship between this function and the four basic functions. We said previously that the summarization function is superimposed on the basic functions. Actually, it is intertwined with the basic functions, because in the mature accounting systems we find the summarizing process going on at all stages and levels of accounting. 

Summarization at the Original Record Level 

At the original record or daybook level, for instance, we find that the figures are summarized by the cash register before the results are read. A modern cash register may produce the figures for total cash sales or for cash sales broken down by merchandise lines. In a drug store having a very simple application of the cash register, the daily tape shows the total cash sales for prescriptions, soda fountain, tobacco and cigarettes, cosmetics, proprietary medicines, and so forth. In this case, the tape both sorts and totals. 

The bookkeeper never handles the actual details of the transac- 171

tions at all. His journal entry concerns only the daily totals for each department. Actually the modern cash register can produce summarized figures for sales on account, cash received on account, cash paid out, sales taxes collected, and the like. In each case the actual figure is produced merely by the punching of the buttonsthe original record function of the daybook. Then the cogs and wheels take over and perform the highly important practical task of summarization. Thus, at the end of the day the bookkeeper has five or ten figures to journalize instead of the thousands of figures for a full day's operation.

The summarization function assumes many forms, with or without mechanical aids. Time tickets, requisitions, receiving documents, expense vouchers, deposit tickets, and many other original records pass through various steps and stages of summarization before they reach the journal stage. Some of the best illustrations of summarization at the original record stage can be found in punched card and tabulating procedures. A complete study of the original record function would involve the area of office management, which is closely related to accounting at the original record stage.

Summarization at the Ledger and Statements Levels 

Although the point is not stressed in the previous discussions of the ledgers and statements, it should be clear that those phases of the work also involved the function of summarization. Each account in the ledger is actually a device for summarizing the monthly or yearly figures and producing totals from the detailed ledger entries. Generally speaking, the total figures, not the individual entries, are significant to management. 

Even though statements are very briefly treated in Chapters VIII and IX, it is obvious that an essential element of statement design is to produce statements that highlight significant subtotal figures and to set them up in matching form in order to portray clearly in the statements those relationships that are most important to management. This handling of the summarizing function is effective at the statement level. In actual practice the income statement and the statement of condition are only two of dozens or hundreds of reports that are produced by the accountant in a large business. Each report involves the function of summariza-

tion. The effectiveness of each report depends largely on whether the accountant clearly appreciates the use which management may make of his summarized figures. 

It is important to visualize the function of summarization as overlying the four basic functions because, in designing an accounting system, it is possible to place certain necessary summarizations earlier or later in the sequence of accounting procedures to gain effectiveness or economy. The problem may be stated another way: if the summarization is poorly handled at the original records phase, the journals may be overburdened with details. If the journals are poorly designed, the ledgers may be overburdened with details which they are not equipped to handle. Poorly designed ledgers make the task of proper statement preparation more difficult, if not impossible.

Summarization at the Journal Level 

The purpose of this chapter is to examine the problem of summarization as it applies to the design of the specialized journals. 

The multicolumnar journal with its special columns is clearly designed to perform a summarizing operation at the journal level. 

The need for intelligent summarization is even more effectively served by splitting the single journal into specialized journals (or books of original entry as they are often called) each dealing with a particular type or types of transactions. 

Incidentally, the use of specialized journals enables several people to work on the journals simultaneously, which may be important as the volume of work grows to exceed the capacity of one person. In some instances, the use of the more highly specialized journals has an added advantage, namely, that persons with little training may be able to maintain them. If the original records are properly planned, the transactions which are sorted, summarized, and routed to a particular special journal are all of one general nature so that the task of entering them becomes fairly routine. Contrast this situation with that in which all the transactions of a business are recorded in a single journal. It should be apparent that a much higher degree of skill is required of the bookkeeper in the latter case. The use of specialized journals produces an economy of operation which closely parallels that made by the use of highly specialized semi-automatic machinery in a

factory where the operator is required to do little more than feed the materials into the machine, press a button, and watch the product emerge.

The special journal saves the bookkeeper's time since less writing is necessary to describe the transaction. For instance, if a sales journal is set up to receive the entry of all sales on account, and only those entries, as is commonly done, the actual entry need not specify "accounts receivable debit" nor "sales credit" because every entry in the book is a debit to accounts receivable and a credit to sales. All that is necessary is the name of the customer and the amount of the sale. Although the writing of a few words may save little time in an isolated case, when the few words are multiplied hundreds of thousands of times the real savings run into many hours and many dollars. 

Planning the Journal System

The decision concerning what journals should be set up and how they should be designed depends on the frequency of each type of transaction, on the circumstances surrounding its occurrence, and on the nature or form of the original record which is available. If possible, the routines should be organized in such a way that the making of the original record is tied into the transaction so closely that each transaction, on its occurrence, causes a record to be made. For instance, a cash sale requires the making of change and making change requires the ringing up of the amount on the cash register. The writing of a check for a disbursement automatically produces a carbon copy of the check, which is the bookkeeper's evidence of the disbursement. An employee cannot be paid except on the evidence of a card with time clock punches on it. The delivery man cannot deliver groceries without having a carbon copy of the sales ticket to give him the name and address of the customer.

Each of these records is part of the original record function. 

Each must be considered carefully because these original records to a considerable extent determine the form of the journals. In each case the bookkeeper who makes the journal entry is working from some specific piece of paper, either an actual original document or some summarizing form. The original record routines must be visualized as a whole, both in organizing the journals in

1 the first place and in running them effectively as part of a complete accounting system.

Naturally the journals which are appropriate for a grocery store are not the same as those for an apartment rental agency, a railroad, or a dental clinic. Every business of course has certain transactions in common, such as cash receipts and cash disbursements, hence a cash receipts journal and a cash disbursements journal are found in virtually every business. However, in each of these books, the specialization in the columns depends on differences in the transactions to be recorded. Occasionally, cash receipts and cash disbursements are combined in one cash journal. 

Most businesses have some journal designed specifically to record revenues. This journal is most often designated the sales journal, although it may be known as a rents received journal or a premium incomes journal or it may have some other appropriately descriptive title. Usually a business has a purchase journal. 

In trading businesses, this journal may record only purchases of merchandise on account. In manufacturing businesses, it is customary to record purchases of raw materials, supplies, and all kinds of expense services in the purchase journal. When a journal of this type is used with certain special filing routines for the detailed records of creditors' accounts, it takes the name "voucher register," although its function is still that of the purchase journal. 

In every system there is a general or sundries journal to pick up the odd transactions which do not qualify for any of the specialized journals. Adjusting and closing entries are always made in the general journal. It may be a simple two-column journal or it may utilize one or more special columns in addition to the basic general debit and general credit columns. 

Flexibility is the keynote for the effective instalation of accounting systems, especially at the journal level, and flexibility of attitude should be the aim of the accountant.

Journal System For a Trading Business 

J. & J. Pilcher Company has a grocery, produce, and meat business, it buys and sells on account, and it delivers. It is representative of the independent, high-class, city market. The accounting system includes general ledgers, customers' ledgers, and creditors' ledgers, and the journals illustrated in Figures 41 through 45.

Note that each of the entries in the sales journal, Figure 41, balances as to debits and credits. Therefore, the totals at the end of the month must balance, and thus the accuracy of each entry is proved with respect to debit and credit equality, accuracy in forwarding totals from page to page, and accuracy of addition. 

Although the debits and credits balance, that fact does not prove either that the sales are entered to the correct customer's account or that each sale is distributed to the proper column in the sales journal.

Obviously the posting of the detailed debits to the customers' accounts should be done each day both to prevent the staff from being overwhelmed with work at the month-end and to have the information in the customers' accounts up-to-date in case some customer wishes to pay his account or raises a question about the amount due. At the end of the month, the totals would be posted in accordance with the content of each column as described at its head. Notice that the posting of the totals can be visualized as a compound journal entry summarizing the month's business in the following manner.

They make similar general journal entries to summarize each of the other special journals. Apparently, they find it desirable to enforce a rule that nothing shall be put into the general ledger, except on the authority of a general journal entry. This practice is considered unnecessarily roundabout, and it is recommended that the totals be posted directly from the special journals to the general ledger. The figures in parentheses beneath the totals in the Pilcher Sales Journal, Figure 41, seem to indicate that the posting was done in this direct way.

Most of the entries in this journal are usually debits to cash and credits to accounts receivable; it is evident from the description of the business that the rent revenue and the interest income are only incidental and are received infrequently, even though the accountant who designed the system thought that they merited special treatment in separate columns. 

Reflect on this important point: There are always certain cash receipts that occur rarely; therefore, they do not merit special columns. For such items the journal provides the miscellaneous credit column. In the illustration, the business has borrowed $2,000 on a note. This kind of debit might occur only two or three times a year. Other infrequent types of cash receipts might be: the making of additional investments by the proprietors, cash received from the sale of items of used equipment (not to be confused with sales of merchandise), cash refunds received from creditors who had accidentally been overpaid, and so forth. All such items are entered in the cash debit column and in the miscellaneous credit column, and the name of the account to be credited is shown in the broad accounts credited column. It is important to realize that the items in the miscellaneous credit column must be posted individually to the general ledger. For this reason, a posting reference column is found under the miscellaneous credit heading. In it check marks (or page references) will be placed whenever a detailed item is posted. The total of this miscellaneous column is shown as a necessary part of the cross-balancing system but it is not posted, as the letters N.P. shown below the total column indicate.

A special problem arises in connection with the handling of cash sales in journals of this kind since the transaction seems to belong to both the sales book and the cash receipts book. One solution is shown in the illustration. The total of each day's cash sales is handled as though it were a sale on account to a fictitious "Mr. 

Cash" who pays his account later on the same day. Thus an entry is made each day in both the sales journal and the cash receipts journal. If the bookkeeper went through with the fiction of a "Mr. Cash," he would balance the account each day by posting to each side of the Mr. Cash account in the customers' ledger. Being naturally lazy, the smart bookkeeper does not post to the Mr.

The Cash Disbursements Journal 

The cash disbursements journal, Figure 43, is basically similar to the cash receipts journal. In this journal every item is entered in the cash credit column and in one of the debit columns. In addition if the cash payment involves the taking of a discount, the credit for the discount is entered in the purchase discount column. At the bottom of each page and at the end of the month the sum of the two credit column totals must be equal to the sum of the four debit column totals. Most of the entries in this journal are debits to accounts payable with a lesser number of entries in wage and advertising expense. Provision must be made, however, in the miscellaneous debit column for the still less frequent entries, such as cash payments of low activity expenses, the cash purchase of assets, and the cash payment of liabilities other than accounts payable.

Detailed items in the accounts payable column must be posted to the individual creditors' accounts in the creditors' ledger. The detailed items in the miscellaneous debit column and the totals of each of the other columns must be posted to the general ledger. 

In the illustration this posting system is indicated by the check marks and by the account numbers beneath the column totals which are used as posting references.

We might have a cash purchase of merchandise occasionally in a business of this kind. Such a transaction could be handled by entering the debit to the Grocery Purchases, Produce Purchases, or Meat Purchases account in the miscellaneous debit column, or the transaction could be run through the purchase journal and the cash disbursements journal as a purchase on account from "Mr. Cash." This treatment would avoid detailed postings to the purchase accounts and give us cumulative totals within the month for each kind of purchases in the purchase journal.

The Purchase Journal 

Pilcher's purchase journal, as illustrated in Figure 44, is designed to handle not only the purchases on account of the three types of merchandise but also the rather frequent purchase on account of delivery expense items such as gas and oil and garage services. It can also accommodate the purchase on account of less active expense items, such as the lawyer's bill for $200, and the purchase on account of assets, such as furniture and fixtures. Space is provided near the right margin as a convenience in listing the account titles of the general ledger. This device operates the same as it would if the amount column were called "miscellaneous." 

Some purchase journals are set up only for purchases of merchandise on account. This form of purchase journal requires other purchases on account to be placed in the general journal. The journal illustrated offers the advantage of bringing together all the purchases on account and of furnishing a single cumulative figure to credit to accounts payable during the month. 

The posting system is again demonstrated by the use of check marks and posting references to the account numbers. All items in the column, accounts payable credit, are posted currently to the individual creditors' accounts in the creditors' ledger. Detailed items in the general (miscellaneous) column are posted individually in the general ledger to accounts named. The totals of the five special columns are posted at the end of the month to their respective accounts in the general ledger. As in all properly designed journals, the column headings specify clearly the debit and credit impact of the postings from the columns.

THE GENERAL JOURNAL 

Pilcher's final journal is the general journal here illustrated as Figure 45. It is designed to accommodate all transactions which do not fit properly into the four specialized journals. This journal, of course, includes adjusting, closing, and retransferring entries at the end of the period and a variety of miscellaneous entries during the period. Some current entries which appear frequently in the general journal are illustrated in Figure 45. Sales returns from account customers, the write-off of uncollectible customers' ac-

counts, the settlement of creditors' accounts by the giving of notes, purchase returns, and uninsured losses by fire are shown. The receipt of notes on account and the correction of many kinds of errors are also shown by entries in the general journal under this system.

The accountant who designed the Pilcher system realized that quite a few entries in the general journal would involve debits to creditors' accounts and credits to customers' accounts; therefore, he provided special columns for these classes of transactions. In so doing, he is taking advantage of the special columns because they give economy in posting in exactly the same way as does the multicolumnar journal discussed in Chapter VIII. If, on analysis, the accountant found that a considerable number of debits to the Bad Debts Expense account or to the Notes Receivable account were developing he could extend the general journal to include columns for these items. It is much more important to grasp this concept of flexibility in the journal system than to know how any particular set of journals is handled. 

In the Pilcher general journal, the detailed items from the accounts payable debit and accounts receivable credit columns are posted currently to the individual creditor's and customer's accounts in their respective subsidiary ledgers. This posting should be kept up-to-date for the same reason that postings from the other journals to creditors' and customers' accounts should be kept up-to-date, that is, to provide management with finger-tip facts regarding its relations with all outside parties at all times. 

The rather close analysis which we have given the journals illustrated in this chapter demonstrates clearly the principles involved in the journal function.

The special columns provided for the sales and purchases of the three lines of merchandise are clearly a recognition of the importance to management of such segregated information. There are, of course, many "grocery stores" having three venture businesses which account for all purchases and sales in the single venture form. Since we know that price margins, turnovers, and other significant management problems vary greatly between these three lines of activity, we can readily see how the single venture form of accounting for an enterprise that is really a triple venture would leave management greatly handicapped.

The design of the journals depends to a considerable extent on the nature of the original transactions and on the availability of the information regarding those transactions at the point of their entry into the journals. Are there actually enough cash disbursements for advertising expenses to justify the use of the special column in the cash disbursements journal? Are there enough purchases of delivery expense items on account to justify that special column in the purchases journal? If the company's advertising is handled by an agency, it is likely that the bookkeeper receives the entire advertising expense in the form of one monthly bill from the agency. If the company contracts with a nearby garage for the maintenance of its delivery trucks, it probably receives weekly or monthly bills for a large part of its delivery expense. In this case the delivery expense charges can be handled more economically as individual entries in the column for general ledger items in the purchase journal.

The previous discussion indicates that the journals occupy a central position in the process of summarization. Therefore, it is clear that in designing the journals the accountant must watch the frequency with which items appear in the original records, and the final use which management is expected to make of the figures, if his system is to be both economical and effective. 

Posting from the Original Documents

An understanding of formal journalizing and posting, as demonstrated in the discussion of the J. & J. Pilcher Company, Figures 41-45, is basic to an understanding of the relationship between the control account and the subsidiary ledger. If there is a considerable volume of transactions, however, and especially if bookkeeping machines are used, it is more economical to post to the subsidiary ledgers directly from the original documents and to enter into the journals only the daily totals of the homogeneous batches of transactions. Basically, the control relationships are still the same. Every transaction, debit or credit, which gets into the subsidiary ledger also must be entered in the control account on the same side. At the end of each month the agreement of control and subsidiary ledgers must be verified by checking the total of the subsidiary account balances against the control account in the general ledger.

In the practical daily operation of an accounts receivable system for a merchandising business, each day's batch of sales slips are assembled (usually on the day after the sale), alphabetized, and run up on an adding machine. The adding machine total is entered in the sales book, and the sales slips are handed to the posting clerk for posting to the customers' individual accounts. If a bookkeeping machine is used to post the detailed items, the machine automatically accumulates a total of the debits posted. At the end of the posting for the day, the total from the machine is compared with the original total provided by the adding machine tape to prove that the posting clerk has entered the correct amounts. This proof operation does not indicate that the items were posted to the correct accounts, that the old balances were correctly picked up, or that compensating errors were not committed. 

If the posting is not a machine operation, a parallel proof can be established by having someone, preferably not the posting clerk, run an adding machine tape for the items newly posted in the subsidiary accounts. The tape total may be compared with the pre-listed adding machine total. This method of proof is limited to the fact that the total new debits equal the total of the sales slips. It is not possible to prove by this method either that the items were posted to the proper accounts or that the accounts are free from compensating errors.

The description of the posting of the debits to accounts receivable can obviously be adapted to the posting of the credits. Cash receipt tickets and sales return slips may be merged alphabetically in one group and pre-listed on an adding machine, or they may be separately pre-listed and posted. Such totals must then agree with the bookkeeping machine total of credits posted or with a review run of the new credits in the subsidiary accounts. 

To facilitate the review runs and the checking for errors, if necessary, the subsidiary accounts which have been active in any posting run are usually "offset" in the tray or file drawer after they have received their postings until the day's work has been proved. 

With slight adjustments the method previously described can be adapted to posting the details of account purchases to the creditors' ledger and to the accounts payable control account, as well as to any other control and subsidiary ledger system. In each case the nature of the source document and the timing and routine by

which it becomes available must be considered carefully. The needs of management with respect to the subsidiary and control account information must carefully be considered. For instance, although information concerning accounts receivable and accounts payable must be kept up-to-date, a subsidiary expense ledger might be posted only once a week if the volume is such that weekly posting is more economical. Although information about expenses must be accurate, usually such information is timely if it is up-todate at the end of each week or month. 

If the sales account is broken down into a relatively few departments, the sales tickets can be totaled by departments on the adding machine, the totals can be proved against the total of the charges to accounts receivable, and the entry to the special columns in the sales journals can be made from these adding machine tapes. On certain bookkeeping machines the departmental totals are obtained as a by-product from the posting of the accounts receivable. This method is practicable if there are not more than ten or twelve departments. For the full-fledged department store with many departments, other techniques must be developed to keep the charges to accounts receivable in agreement with the credits to sales.

2. Rule a set of specialized journals for W. & B. Linde whose business comprises a shoe store with departments for men's, women's, and children's shoes.

From the data given, you should be able to visualize the frequency of the transactions and the requirements of the management. You are to provide the special columns desirable for the journals. It is suggested that the five journals commonly used in merchandising be adopted, but you are to devise the columnar arrangement within each journal. Be sure to make provision for folio references in all columns from which the detail has to be posted. 

When your journals are complete, write explicit instructions for posting the detailed items and the totals of all columns. If either details or totals are not to be posted, you must specifically so state. 

DESCRIPTION OF THE BUSINESS 

The annual sales volume is about $350,000. About two-thirds of the sales are made on account to 4500 charge customers. All purchases are made on account from twenty-five or thirty suppliers, the terms are uniformly n/30, and shipments are received three or four times per week. There are six full-time and three part-time employees; they are paid on a salary basis at an hourly rate. The management considers the proportion of cash and account sales in each department important management information. The expenses of highest frequency are wages, advertising, and utilities; all expenses are paid in cash as soon as the bills are received. New equipment is infrequently purchased. Cash receipts other than on account and

Chapter IX, Problem 2 (continued) 

for cash sales are very infrequent. Each department has its own modern cash register. No notes are taken or given. Not more than three or four bad debts are written off each year. The salesmen are so well trained that sales returns are very infrequent. 

SUGGESTION: You might compare the conditions outlined for 

W. & B. Linde with the conditions obtaining in the J. & J. Pilcher business in the text; you may wish to use the Pilcher journals as a point of departure in designing the journals required by this problem. 

3. A. Dwyer runs a women's apparel store. The company buys and sells on account. The annual sales volume is about $300,000. The books, as presented herewith, are closed annually on the last day of 

February. You take charge as bookkeeper on February 27 and are to complete the necessary work for the year. You are assured by the accountant who has checked the books that everything is in balance as you take over and all the posting has been brought up to date. 

The sales on account, the purchases on account, and the cash receipts and the cash payments on account are received in sufficient volume to justify the posting of these entries from the original documents with their totals entered daily in the journals. The system uses an Expense Control account in the general ledger, with all detailed accounts in the expense subsidiary ledger. Postings to the expense ledger are made from the special expense columns in the cash disbursements book, the purchases book, and the general journal. 

The few accounts receivable and accounts payable entries in the general journal are posted from the journal rather than directly. 

The folio columns ruled in the journals indicate the columns from which the detailed items are to be posted. 

After the details and the totals have all been posted, take a trial balance of the general ledger and draw up schedules of the balances of the three subsidiary ledgers to prove that these ledgers agree with their control accounts.

Chapter IX, Problem 4 

4. Brenner Incorporated (whose books are shown on the following pages) operates a small department store, twenty-three departments. 

With these journals the corporation also uses a general ledger posted by hand and customers' and creditors' ledgers that are posted by machine. These subsidiary ledgers are all posted directly from original documents, so that the journals receive only daily totals for these transactions.

Cash sales are entered daily in the sales register and cash receipts book. The cash sales memo columns are used to preserve the balance in each book and yet to keep the cash sales out of the accounts receivable system.

The total of all sales returns (including cash sales returns) are entered in the sales return register each day. The memo columns for cash transactions are designed to keep the accounts receivable system "clean."

The general ledger has thirty or forty expense accounts classified according to the object of the expenditure. These expenses are allocated to departments on a statistical basis when the monthly statements are made. These allocations are never recognized in the ledger accounts.

For each of the twenty-three departments, the general ledger shows inventory, sales, sales returns, and purchase accounts. There are no departmental purchase returns accounts. Actually purchase returns are rather rare; when they do appear, they are handled in the journals and posted in the ledgers as negative purchases. Management does not consider them to be important.

Purchase discounts are handled in a single account in the ledger, but they are allocated to departments at each monthly closing by a statistical recapitulation from the underlying documents. 

DIRECTIVE: Write explicit instructions for posting the journals of Brenner Incorporated. Give particular attention to the detail items and the totals of the columns identifying them by the key letters.
