CHAPTER X  

THE VOUCHER register system can best be understood by considering anew the problems of recording purchases and of controlling cash payments. Our purpose in reviewing these problems is to achieve greater economy of effort by being realistic about certain practical difficulties. 

The Practical Difficulties in Recording Purchases 

The term purchases, as it is used in modern business, especially in manufacturing, has a broader significance than merely buying merchandise for resale. It also refers to the buying of expense materials and services, raw materials, parts and partial assemblies, plant assets, and so forth. All of these purchases are similar in one important respect-they all cause the expenditure of cash. Management views the complicated process of purchasing from a practical standpoint. It seeks a guaranty that each expenditure shall be entirely legitimate and proper. When we realize that hundreds of persons may be involved in purchasing-requisitioning, asking for bids, ordering, receiving, checking quantities, descriptions, and prices, and in the final preparation and signing of checks-it becomes clear that the whole process requires a stronger, more centralized control than is available in the systems which are discussed in the earlier chapters of this text. 

The Pattern of the Purchasing Process 

In visualizing the complications of the purchasing process, we can consider that the merchandise purchase sets the basic pattern. 

The proprietor, or someone acting for him, orders the goods. This request for goods results in an actual document, the purchase order. The original of this order is sent to the supplier, and a carbon copy of it remains in the office. The supplier sends to the purchaser an invoice describing the goods and stating prices, terms, amount due, and dates-invoice, order, shipment, and so forth. When the goods are received, we have three sets of "facts" 207

that must be compared with each other, the purchase order, the invoice, and the actual goods. This basic operation is necessary to establish the propriety of paying the invoice. The purchase order, the invoice, and the actual goods must agree in quantity, description, quality, price, timing, terms, and other pertinent conditions. 

The purchasing process in the preceding outline may seem ob vious and simple, but in practice each of the three "facts" may be complicated in itself. The purchase order, for instance, may be a standing contract under which a supplier forwards batches of materials either as they become available or in response to specific telephone requests. Each batch may be sold at the market price prevailing at the time of shipment. Possibly the specifications for the goods ordered are written in highly technical language or perhaps the goods must conform to a set of blueprints, and either the specifications or the blueprints may be subject to changes. 

The goods may be received in partial shipments or they may have to be subjected to physical and chemical laboratory tests of quality. The tests of quality may have to be performed by outside agencies which arbitrate quality-price relationships between the buyer and seller. If advertising, legal, or accounting services instead of material goods are being purchased, the inspection of the "purchase" may involve the subjective judgments of and approvals by department heads or executives. If a piece of machinery or other long-lived asset is purchased, final acceptance may have to await the lapsing of a guaranty period, or the completion of a series of performance tests.

The invoice itself may vary in form and in the degree of detail appearing on it. Charges for prepaid freight, packing, and handling may be included, and the charges may or may not agree with the understanding of the buyer. The descriptions of the goods given on the invoice may not tally exactly with those given on the purchase order, but the goods received may be acceptable substitutes for those ordered. This change would require the okeh of a department head or a technical expert qualified to make such a judgment.

It is obvious that variations often may occur between the documents and the "facts" they represent. Hence it can easily be understood that careful checking is necessary before the liability for payment is recognized. Careful planning and organization are re-

quired to provide adequate checking. The voucher system is the result of such planning and organization. 

The purchase-expenditure voucher is essentially a check-sheet on which we bring together all the data necessary to authorize an expenditure and to effect its proper disposition in the accounts (see Figure 46). Physically it may be a sheet of paper, a manila card, or an envelope. In any case it is a printed form with spaces in which to insert the essential data. The basic information is copied from the creditor's invoice on the standardized form. Certain spaces on the form must be initialed by the persons assuming responsibility for: issuance of purchase order, acceptance of the quantities received, the technical quality, the mathematical calculations, the correctness of the terms of contract, the planned date of payment, and possibly other items. Generally there is a provision for the signature of some person with the authority to take final responsibility for the validity of all the checking and initiating.

Finally the voucher must show the accounts to be debited. The person who makes this analysis may be the “voucher clerk” or an assistant controller, but he must be someone who knows all the account classifications and the system of management reports. 

His judgment as shown on this section of the voucher determines the final disposition of the charge. Any error that he might make in the disposition of a charge is not likely to be discovered afterwards. The titles and the numbers of accounts that are used frequently may be printed in this section, but additional blank spaces must be provided in which to indicate charges to less active accounts.

The Voucher Register 

In the voucher system the purchase journal, with a few changes, becomes the voucher register (see Figure 47). This register performs the summarizing function of the journal. Like the purchase journal, it provides a column for the newly recognized liabilities, although the new column and the corresponding account in the general ledger are usually called Vouchers Payable instead of Accounts Payable. It also provides columns for the distribution of the debits based on the volume of transactions and the account classification. Sometimes it is claimed that the voucher register

has a unique feature in that expense and asset purchases may be run through it, but these transactions can be handled in the purchase journal (Chapter IX). It is true, however, that the voucher system is used largely by manufacturing corporations whose purchases are more varied than the purchases of the typical trading business. Thus the voucher register may be identified with the need for more complicated expense accounting. 

Because expenses must be extensively classified in manufacturing companies, expense controls must be more refined in such concerns than in other businesses. One development, for instance, might be to divide the expense control account into three separate control accounts, one each for Administrative Expense, Selling Expense, and Manufacturing Expense. Each of these accounts would control a separate section of the expense ledger, and each would be represented by a special column in the voucher register. 

Each voucher is entered on a separate line in the voucher register. In addition to the usual columns previously described, the voucher register provides columns in which the final disposition of each voucher may be indicated. If the voucher is paid by check, the check number and date are entered on its line in the "disposition" columns. Other possible dispositions of the voucher may be to settle it by giving a note, to consolidate it with other vouchers due the same creditor for payment by a single check, to cancel it partially or wholly when the goods are returned to the vendor, to make a partial payment and issue a new voucher for the unpaid balance, and so forth. Such miscellaneous dispositions of the voucher are noted by symbol in the check number column. Whatever disposition is made of the voucher, the register must record the entire transaction.

To emphasize the central importance of the voucher register, the bookkeeping system employing such a method is referred to as a "voucher register system," although the terminology "voucher system" is probably to be preferred.

Since the vouchers when they are paid or otherwise settled are checked off in the disposition columns, it is a simple matter to identify at any time those vouchers that are in force as liabilities. 

At the end of each month the total of the unpaid vouchers should be checked against the balance in the vouchers payable account in the general ledger.

11 

The Voucher Filing System 

After each voucher is entered in the register, it is filed in the "Unpaid Vouchers File." In the usual system, all vouchers are filed in accordance with the expected date of payment. Thus regardless of its date of origin, each voucher is found in a batch with a similar payment date, and each batch works its way forward in the file until it is in the front position on the day it is to be paid. Some of the vouchers in the day's batch may have been in the file for thirty or sixty days representing purchases that were made one or two months ago on terms of n/30 or n/60. Other vouchers may have been in the file only for five or ten days, if their terms are cash discount of 3/5 or 2/10 as is common. 

As the payment date arises the person authorized to sign the check has the responsibility for a final "audit" of the propriety of paying the voucher. All the significant supporting papers usually are filed with the voucher so that they are available for this final inspection. The design of the voucher as an envelope facilitates filing the supporting papers together. Such an envelope is usually known as the "voucher jacket."

When a voucher is paid, that fact is noted on the face of the voucher as well as in the disposition column of the register. The voucher and its supporting documents are then filed in the "Paid 

Vouchers" file. Note that this refiling accomplishes exactly what the debit to the creditors' account accomplishes-that is, evidence of the fact that the liability has been paid. Sometimes instead of the final filing of the paid voucher the vendor's invoice is filed alphabetically by name in a separate file. Since the voucher is filed by number, this double filing plus the record of the transaction in the voucher register comprises a complete and serviceable record of the entire transaction, particularly when each file and entry are fully cross-referenced.

The Voucher Check System 

A variation of the straight voucher system has become very popular. It combines the voucher and the check with which the voucher is paid. The form has a double fold; the front carries the check and a summarized copy of the pertinent data from the invoice. A carbon imprint of the check and invoice data appear

on the front of the second sheet. An analysis of the charges appears on the back of the second sheet. 

The completed form is filed in the unpaid vouchers file until the payment date arrives. At the payment date, the check receives its signature. The check and the summary of the invoice, that is the entire front sheet is detached and sent to the creditor, and the back sheet is filed in the paid vouchers file. The check itself bears a notation to the effect that it is in payment of the items noted on the attached summary of the invoice. Thus, the creditor's endorsement of the check constitutes an acknowledgment of payment for the specific debt. When the check is returned through the bank and filed among the paid checks, the company has a complete record of the transaction: the invoice, the voucher, and the paid check all filed and cross-referenced-the voucher facts spread on the voucher register and the check listed in the check register (cash disbursements book).

Dispensing with the Creditors' Ledger Much of what has been said about the voucher register system may seem to involve more detailed work than the purchase journal system did. To some extent there is more work in the voucher system, but it is the result of the complicated structure of large scale business rather than the result of the disadvantages of the system.

The voucher system does have the advantage of economy: it enables us to dispense entirely with the creditors' ledger. The unpaid vouchers file and the files of paid vouchers and invoices, previously described, perform every necessary function that the detailed creditors' ledger performs. 

The only disadvantage of the voucher system is that it does not provide a highly summarized history of the relations with any particular creditor such as the individual creditor's ledger provides, however, the voucher system does provide an alphabetical-byvendors file of invoices. Since management does not need the summarized history of its creditor relationships frequently, we may assume that this disadvantage of the voucher system is not usually a serious one. There is of course no reason why an accounting system cannot embrace a creditors' ledger in addition to the voucher register system, if management considers that the histories.

of creditor accounts are important and desirable in spite of the obvious duplication of records and the additional expense. 

Miscellaneous Disbursements and the Voucher Register System 

In practice the voucher register system is usually expanded to include all disbursements, even those that are not the result of purchases in the usual sense. For instance, the weekly payroll is run through the voucher system as though the services of the employees were being bought on account from a creditor called "Mr. 

Payroll," or "Paymaster." In this case the voucher is never actually filed in the unpaid vouchers file but is paid as soon as it is completed. Since there is no purchase order and no creditor's invoice, the records of the personnel department act as authorizing documents relative to pay-rates, and the timeclock records, the time tickets from within the plant, and finally the formal payroll support the propriety of the payment. Usually one check is issued for the amount of the entire payroll. This check is deposited to the credit of a special payroll account, against which the detailed payroll checks are written. The production of the special payroll checks, with their stubs showing the specific deductions before net take-home pay, requires a complicated routine. With the machine technique for writing the checks, the formal payroll can be produced as an automatic by-product, and this payroll furnishes the authority for the master pay voucher and check. 

The payment of a promissory note is likewise run through the voucher system. In this case the bank's notice that the note is due, initialed by the company's treasurer, is the formal authority for making out the voucher. In effect the voucher debits the Notes 

Payable and Interest Expense accounts and credits the Vouchers

Payable account. The entry for the check requires a debit to the 

Vouchers Payable account and a credit to the Cash in Bank account.

The payment of a dividend requires a voucher based on the minutes of the directors' meeting at which the dividend was declared. The voucher entry debits the Dividends Paid or the Surplus or Retained Earnings account and credits the Vouchers Payable account. The check entry again requires a debit to the 

Vouchers Payable account and a credit to the Cash in Bank account.

These illustrations serve to indicate how miscellaneous disbursements may be visualized as purchases in order to funnel them through the centralized single checking process of the voucher system.

Petty Cash Routines 

So far as feasible all disbursements are made by check because it is much easier to safeguard payments by check than payments in currency. However, most businesses find that there are still small payments that must be made in currency. These small currency payments are integrated with the voucher system through what is known as the imprest petty cash fund system.

Initially, a voucher is made for a round amount, perhaps $500; it is charged to a new general ledger account called Petty Cash. 

The check for this voucher is payable to the petty cashier who is the custodian of the fund. This custodian cashes the check, puts the money into a petty cash drawer, to which, preferably, he alone has a key, and is ready for business. As occasion arises, the petty cashier pays small amounts for postage, express, C.O.D.'s, minor supply purchases, and the like, and he takes receipts for such small payments. These receipts may be sales tickets, invoices or bills of the supplier, which are marked “paid” by the person receiving the cash, or they may be the company's own petty cash voucher forms filled out to describe the reason for each payment and marked “paid" by the one receiving the cash. When his fund is low, the petty cashier summarizes the payments which have been made in a "petty cash reimbursement request." This request becomes the authority for a voucher and a check to replenish his fund. The voucher charges the expense accounts shown on the summary and credits the Vouchers Payable account. The check entry debits the Vouchers Payable account and credits the Cash in Bank account. The Petty Cash account remains at the amount for which it was originally established unless an increase or a decrease in it becomes advisable at some future time. 

In effect, this procedure places the small currency payments under the protective features of the formal voucher system. The petty cash system assumes the reliability of the petty cashier. He could, if he were dishonest, pilfer small amounts by raising the figures on some of the very informal receipts which he takes or

1 by including entirely bogus receipts with forged signatures in his requests for reimbursements. Some businesses find it necessary to have several or many petty cash funds, possibly one at each branch or plant or cafeteria line. These funds can be controlled by one account in the general ledger called "Petty Cash Funds," but memorandum records must be kept to show the amount for which each petty cashier is accountable.

A special situation arises at the end of an accounting period when the receipts in the petty cash drawer rightfully should be charged to the expense accounts of the period just coming to a close. One method of debiting the expense accounts requires the petty cashier to ask for a special reimbursement of expenses to date, even though the fund is not low. This method is easy for the bookkeeper, but it requires the writing of a voucher and the issuance of a check which can be avoided by another method of handling the situation.

The second method is to make a general journal entry for the petty cash expenses to date without asking for reimbursement in cash at the time. The following entry might be made.

Cleaning Expense Postage Expense 

....

Typewriter Repairs Expense. 

Merchandise (C.O.D.'s) 

Petty Cash ... 12.75 10.00 16.50 32.30 71.55 

Note that this entry would reduce the Petty Cash account in the general ledger. Presumably, it is desirable to keep the Petty Cash account and the fund at its formal amount. When the fund is being reimbursed in the subsequent accounting period a week or two later, therefore, it would be necessary to make the following entry in the voucher register: 

Petty Cash

Cleaning Expense 

Postage

Delivery Expense Office Expense 

... 

Vouchers Payable ...

General Administration Expense. 

71.55 14.30 21.00 135.00 82.00 143.00 466.85 

The debit to Petty Cash reinstates the full amount of the Petty Cash account. The petty cash disbursements made in the new

accounting period debit the expense accounts affected. The check when drawn for $466.85 reimburses the petty cashier both for the $71.55 which he had spent prior to the closing of the year and for the $395.30 spent since the opening of the new year. 

Purchase Returns and the Voucher Register 

The handling of purchase returns and allowances in the voucher register is somewhat awkward. If many such transactions are anticipated, they might constitute a determining objection to the voucher system. The occasional return or allowance is best handled in the voucher register by entering the amount as a negative item (in red or circled) on the same line as the voucher itself. If the negative items are comparatively insignificant, the totals of the columns, vouchers payable credit, and the affected distribution column may then be shown net, and so posted. If the returns and allowances are more significant, separate totals may be shown for the plus and minus items at the foot of the columns. 

These separate totals may then be posted as follows: the minus amount in the vouchers payable column as a debit to the Vouchers Payable account; the negative item in the distribution column either as a credit to the expense account involved or as a credit to the purchase returns and allowances account if the item was originally debited to purchases.

In actual accounting practice, since the voucher itself is presumably still in the unpaid vouchers file, the reduction of the liability is noted on it in order to make certain that payment is made at the net amount.

Partial Payments of Vouchers 

Under the voucher system each purchase is regarded as a separate contract and is paid for separately. Sometimes, it may be desirable to make a partial payment on a voucher. In this case the check bears a specific notation, "in partial payment of voucher #876." A corresponding notation is made on the voucher itself, "partial payment of $96.00 made hereon by check #9734." The voucher then remains in the unpaid vouchers file in a date position which brings it to the front of the file on its settlement date. 

A notation must also be made in the disposition columns of the voucher register indicating that partial payment has been made.

R A more formal technique for handling the partial payment must be used if the system combines the voucher and the check. 

Under this technique the original voucher is canceled and marked “canceled" in the voucher register. Two new vouchers are then made out, one for the amount of the partial payment and one for the postponed balance. The new voucher for the partial payment is paid and filed in the paid vouchers file; the voucher for the balance is filed in the unpaid vouchers file under its new pay. ment date. The original canceled voucher is filed in the paid vouchers file with a clear notation of the facts, including the numbers of the two substitute vouchers, on its face. The voucher register disposition columns show the original voucher as "canceledreissued, see #9879 and #9880." The two new vouchers are entered on lines of their own in proper voucher number sequence. 

The Check Register 

The cash disbursements journal used in conjunction with a voucher register may be very simple in form. Since all payments funnel through the voucher system, all checks written require debits to the Vouchers Payable account. Thus the need for the distribution-of-debits columns is eliminated; the cash journal is reduced to a very simple form showing only three columns, one for cash credit, one for purchase discount credit, and one for vouchers payable debit. Such a simple cash disbursements journal is commonly called a check record or check register. If cash discounts are not taken, the check record can be kept in a book with a single column. At the end of the month the total of the check record is debited to Vouchers Payable and credited to Cash in 

Bank in the general ledger.

Illustration of Voucher and Voucher Register 

The voucher illustrated in Figure 46 brings out the points described in the text. On the front it carries the formal data regarding the creation of the liability and indicates the steps that must be taken to verify its proper payment. Although the details shown on the voucher vary widely in the items specified, the voucher illustrated in Figure 46 is fairly typical of those in use in manufacturing concerns. An important variation in the voucher often concerns the amount of detail which management desires to have

shown regarding the nature of the materials or services purchased. 

Since such information is available on the invoice, it is a wasteful duplication of time and effort to copy all the details on the voucher. On the other hand, it may be desirable to show more detail on the voucher if the voucher and invoice are filed separately than if they are filed together. 

The accounts to be debited (charged) for the items shown on the face of the voucher are identified on the reverse side of the voucher. To facilitate this task the numbers and titles of those accounts which are most frequently used are printed on the voucher. The numbers and titles of those accounts less frequently used are written in the space provided. A business having a very limited classification of accounts might have the entire list printed on the voucher, but the form of voucher illustrated in Figure 46 is obviously more useful for a large business with hundreds or thousands of accounts.

It should be understood that any one voucher receives charges only for one account, or at best only for a few accounts. For instance, the voucher for a typical raw material purchase (a frequent transaction in manufacturing businesses) shows a charge only to account #141 or possibly a charge to this account and to #145 Freight-In, if the freight is prepaid by the vendor and is billed along with the purchase price of the actual raw material. The voucher made out for the weekly payroll might show charges to accounts #501, #502, #503, #504, #551, #581, and #582, that is, to all the salary and wage accounts. 

Account Classification and the Voucher System 

The relationships between the voucher, the form of the voucher register, and the procedure for making entries will be clearer to you if you visualize them against the background of the related classification of accounts. Such a classification, if it is well designed, not only aids in the routine work of recording, but also guarantees to management that available information is arranged in useful categories.

The numbers and titles of the accounts, shown on the reverse side of the voucher in Figure 46 and on the illustrated voucher register in Figure 47, are taken from the following classification or chart of accounts shown in Figure 48. Although this classifica-

tion is too limited to serve adequately in any business but a very small one, it illustrates the systematic numbering of accounts and the overall design of a classification.

It should be clear that debits to almost any account in the classification might have to be made as the result of a purchase made through the voucher system. Both the distribution section of the voucher and the voucher register, however, have been designed for maximum economy since they provide printed headings and special columns only for those accounts which receive the most frequent entries. It should be noted that the printed account titles and numbers on the voucher correspond to the special columns for the distribution of charges in the voucher register. Thus, it is feasible and safe to use the account numbers as column headings.

The voucher register must provide, as it does at the extreme right, for any account that might possibly receive a debit. These accounts would be written on the voucher by the voucher clerk. 

Since these accounts are less frequently used, the office personnel would be less familiar with them, and therefore, the voucher register requires that, as a safety precaution, both the number and the complete title be noted. A well designed accounting system is characterized by many skillful details such as this safety precaution.

CHAPTER XI  

THE PERIODIC cycle of accounting, as it has been presented in this text, may be visualized as a series of tasks: journalizing and posting the current entries of the period, journalizing and posting the special end-of-the-period adjustments, drawing up the statements, and journalizing and posting the closing entries. In a technical sense this cycle is entirely complete and self-contained. Technically, it is the complete story of the accountant's work. 

Practically, however, there is another tool, the work sheet, which the accountant always finds useful, in addition to the technical accounting framework with which we are already familiar. 

The work sheet is not concerned with the current entries of the period, but it is an attempt to gather together on one spread sheet all the special tasks incident to the end of the period. This method of bringing together all the loose ends serves many useful purposes in practical accounting, and incidentally, it assists the student in consolidating his comprehension of the whole process. 

In form, the work sheet has 8, 10, 12, or more columns for the amounts, with space at the left side for the account names. The space for the names of the accounts and the two adjacent columns is used for the pre-adjusting trial balance which is developed from the accounts in the general ledger. This trial balance serves the purposes previously discussed in Chapter IV. 

The next two columns are generally called simply "adjustments." In them the accountant expresses in concise form all the "adjustments" necessary to convert the raw material of the trial balance figures into figures ready for meaningful statements. 

These "adjustments" include the correction of errors, the making of certain special end-of-the-period entries such as depreciation, as well as the technical adjustments needed to correct the trial balance figures for accruals and deferrals of expenses and revenues. Sometimes elements of the closing entries creep into the 243

adjustment columns, although it is better to keep these elements out of the adjustments in order to present a clearer picture. 

After all necessary adjustments have been made, the major task still to be performed on the work sheet is the classification of the trial balance accounts into income statement and statement of condition items. This task is performed in two more pairs of columns, one pair for each statement. The two columns devoted to the income statement sort its items into plus (or income) items and minus (or expense) items to make possible the calculation of the net income (the difference between the totals of the two columns). The two columns devoted to the statement of condition are used to sort its items into the asset and equity classifications, although for convenience it is customary to show such asset contra items as the Allowance for Depreciation in the equity column. 

For the same reason any equity contra items would be shown in the asset column, but this convention should cause no difficulty once it has been pointed out. 

Thus, we require eight columns for the basic tasks on the work sheet, although other columns may be added for other purposes. 

Various forms of work sheets are used for special occasions. The basic work sheet, which we are studying, is used by the accountant at the end of an accounting period. It aids him in gathering his adjustments together in preparation for the making of the statements and the formal closing of the ledger accounts. After we understand this basic use of the work sheet, it is comparatively easy to extend our thinking to the use of the work sheet for the preparation of interim statements and for other more or less specialized purposes.

The Basic Work Sheet 

The eight-column work sheet is illustrated in Figure 49; it demonstrates the techniques used. 

Note that the pre-adjusting trial balance taken from the ledger on December 31 proves that the ledger is in balance. Entries in the adjustment columns should be traced one by one through the key letters in the light of the following data which called for them. (a) It was discovered that a transaction for $250 on December 17 had been credited to Accounts Payable when it should have been credited to Notes Payable. Note that the adjustment (a) in

lines 14 and 15 debits Accounts Payable and credits Notes Payable. This adjustment reduces Accounts Payable and increases 

Notes Payable. 

(b) It was discovered that a typewriter purchased on December 27 for $160 had been charged to Office Expense instead of to Furniture and Fixtures. Adjustment (b) on lines 12 and 22 removes the $160 from the Office Expense account and places it in the Furniture and Fixtures account. 

Notice that adjustments (a) and (b) both correct errors. Public accountants frequently have to make adjustments of this kind to correct errors which they discover in auditing the books. The company's own accountant also makes such adjustments if, in reviewing the work of the period, he uncovers errors. (c) The accountant calculated the depreciation on the delivery equipment at 20% of cost, $8,200, or $1,640 for the year. Note that the credit for adjustment (c) is recorded in the Allowance for Depreciation of Delivery Equipment, line 11, whereas the debit is recorded in a new account, Depreciation Expense, and appears on line 29. (d) The accountant calculated the depreciation on furniture and fixtures at 10% of the $14,000 cost. Depreciation on the new typewriter purchased December 27 and used for four days is disregarded as inconsequential. Adjustment (d) is closely parallel to adjustment (c). (e) After reviewing carefully the business experience and the present status of the collections, the proprietor and the accountant decided that the losses on bad debts for the period would probably be 3/4 of 1% of the sales for the year, or $1,890. Therefore, the Bad Debts Expense account, line 31, is set up to receive the debit. The credit is recorded in the Allowance for Bad Debts line 

3. The allowance as it now stands indicates that there are presumed to be $700 of bad accounts which originated prior to 1955 still on the books and $1,890 which originated in 1955. The presumed value of the accounts receivable is $16,500 less $2,500 or $13,910.

Note that adjustments (c), (d), and (e) are similar in that the accountant has writen off the cost or value of assets in accordance with his judgment as to their expirations. Although the value of these items may be changing day by day throughout the period,

management is satisfied to have the book figures adjusted at the end of specific periods. (f) On checking payroll records, the accountant discovered that the employees had earned $230 in wages since the prior pay day. Adjustment (f) adds $230 to the Wage Expense account on line 23 and recognizes the liability to employees in the Accrued 

Expenses (wages) account on line 32. (g) The accountant estimated the electricity consumed in December to be $125. The company will be billed about January 10, and the bill will be paid about January 20. On lines 18 and 33 the adjustment debits Utilities Expense and credits Accrued Expenses (utilities) for the amount. This entry is parallel in its effect to adjustment (f). (h) The bonds owned have been earning interest since the prior interest payment date, November 1, at the rate of 4%. The amount earned, $60, is debited to Accrued Income (interest) and credited to Interest Income by adjustment (h) on lines 34 and 26. (i) The accountant examined the carbon copies of rent receipts given to the tenant of the company and found that the tenant had prepaid his rent for January, $300. The adjustment reduces the balance in the Rent Income account by a debit and recognizes the liability for the unearned rent by a credit to Deferred Income (rent) on lines 25 and 35. (j) The janitor reported that fuel, $180, was still on hand on December 31. This fuel was charged to Fuel Expense when it was purchased. Hence the adjustment on lines 19 and 36 reduces (credits) the Fuel Expense account and debits the Deferred Expenses (fuel) account. (k) Unexpired insurance was found to be $800 when the accountant examined the insurance policies. The adjustment reduces Insurance Expense by a credit and recognizes the unexpired value as an asset (debit) in the Deferred Expenses (insurance) account on lines 20 and 37.

It should be noted that adjustments (f) through (k) are the kind of adjustments which we usually think of as technical adjustments necessitated by the short-cut methods used in handling expenses and incomes during the accounting period. These adjustments result in the recognition of the two assets (Accrued Incomes and Deferred Expenses) and the two liabilities (Accrued Expenses and

ד Deferred Incomes) on the work sheet; these assets and liabilities are the same as those which result when we make entries to adjust the accounts in the ledger itself. 

The second task of the work sheet, as we mentioned previously, is to separate the items of the trial balance which concern the income statement from those which concern the statement of condition. This operation is similar to the closing operation in the ledger itself and it requires the same kind of thinking about the basic relationships of the accounts. All the expenses and revenues are carried over to the columns for the income statement and all the assets and equities to the columns for the statement of condition (which are entitled balance sheet in Figure 49). 

Each item carried to either statement must be carried over at its adjusted value, although the original amounts of such items as Cash and Accounts Receivable, which have received no adjustments, are extended directly to the columns for the statement of condition without change. The Allowance for Bad Debts started with a $700 credit and received another credit of $1,890 in the adjustment column so that it has a total credit of $2,590 when it is extended to the credit column for the statement of condition. The amount of the Notes Receivable account is transferred to the statement of condition without any adjustment. 

The merchandise inventory can be handled in any one of several ways. The method shown is the simplest one. The amount of $35,200 in the Merchandise Inventory account represents the goods on hand at the beginning of the period. Since we assume that these goods were used in producing the sales of the period, we place this amount in the debit column for the income statement. The goods purchased amounting to $175,300 likewise have largely been sold, therefore, this amount is also placed in the debit column for the income statement. Purchase Returns and 

Purchase Discounts are subtractions from Purchases and are entered in the credit column for the income statement. The four amounts which we have gathered in the columns for the income statement would comprise the cost of goods sold if there were no goods left on hand at the end of the period. In addition, the end inventory, must be ascertained by taking a physical inventory. 

The amount of the end inventory, $36,500 in this case, is placed in the credit column for the income statement to show the true

cost of the goods sold. The amount of the end inventory also is placed in the debit column for the statement of condition since this inventory is, of course, an asset. The student should recognize that this process of calculating the cost of goods sold in the columns for the income statement is similar to the process used in calculating this cost in the income statement itself. 

Other methods of handling inventory figures for the beginning and ending of the period utilize the adjustment columns, and these columns can, of course, be made to produce the same final results. Under one method, an adjustment is made debiting (or crediting) the Merchandise Inventory account to adjust or change it to show the ending inventory figure; then a corresponding credit (or debit) is made in a new line on the work sheet called 

Inventory Increase (or Decrease). This inventory increase (or decrease) is extended to the columns for the income statement as a credit (or debit). When this method is used the Merchandise 

Inventory account, as adjusted, is extended directly to the debit column for the statement of condition. 

Continuing down the work sheet we see Bonds Owned and Delivery Equipment, which have had no adjustments, carried over to the debit column for the statement of condition. 

Allowance for Depreciation of Delivery Equipment with a 

$3,000 credit in the trial balance and a $1,640 adjustment credit is extended to the credit column for the statement of condition as $4,640.

Furniture and Fixtures, $14,000, plus its $160 adjustment debit, is extended to the debit column for the statement of condition as $14,160.

The student should trace each of the remaining items across the work sheet as we have traced the items in the preceding paragraphs. Note that if an item has a debit in the trial balance column, and in the adjustments column, both debits are added and their total is extended to the income statement column or to the statement of condition column. Of course, if the item has credits in both the trial balance column and the adjustments column, the credits are added and the total extended. On the other hand if an item has a debit in the trial balance column and a credit in the adjustments column, the two figures are netted (that is, the smaller figure is subtracted from the larger) and the result-

ing total is extended to the appropriate debit or credit column, as the case may be. This process is not arbitrary; it is the same process that we used in making adjustments in the ledger accounts. 

Note that the accounts added on the lines below those for the original trial balance accounts must be identified as belonging in the income statement or statement of condition and must be extended to the appropriate columns, just as the trial balance accounts are indentified and extended. Note that several items which require two lines in the adjustment columns are reduced to a single amount when they are extended to columns for the income statement or statement of condition. 

After all the items have been extended, preliminary totals are taken in the columns for the income statement and the statement of condition. The difference between the two income statement columns is equal to the difference between the two statement of condition columns. These totals prove that the work sheet is mathematically accurate, and incidentally, they provide us with the net income for the period. Figure 49 illustrates a good method of indicating that the work sheet is in balance and of reflecting the figure for the total net income. 

The Statements Drawn From the Work Sheet 

When the work sheet balances as it does in the illustration, Figure 49, the accountant can proceed with assurance to draw up the formal income statement and the statement of condition for presentation to the management even before the actual adjustments have been made in the records. Figures 50 and 51 are the income statement and the statement of condition drawn from the work sheet Figure 49.

Although the work sheet may be used to check the overall accuracy of the statements, it does not aid the accountant with respect to some of the finer points of statement technique. The work sheet in the illustration does not assist the accountant in deciding how to show the bonds owned in the statement of condition. In this instance the accountant decided that, in view of circumstances surrounding the ownership of the bonds, it was improper to show them as current assets on the statement of condition and that they had better be shown in an independent section entitled "investments."

In the income statement the accountant elected to add the rent, interest, and miscellaneous income to the gross margin before subtracting the expenses. He presumably made this decision because he considered that some expenses had been incurred partially in the earning of these incomes. Stated in another way, the total expenses, $68,395, do not pertain purely to the gross margin on sales, that is, they are not exclusively store expenses. If more information had been available, he might have matched the expenses more closely against the incomes to which they pertained. 

This procedure, if it could have been adopted, would be better accounting practice. Obviously, it is not the function of the simple work sheet, as shown in Figure 49, to solve such problems as the form of the income statement. Such problems must be thought out by the accountant in the light of the requirements of each case as the statements themselves are being prepared. 

The Work Sheet and the Formal Closing Entries 

Since the work sheet for Q. Thoron, in Figure 49, was drawn up as of the close of the year 1955, we must visualize the related work of making and posting the journal entries to transfer the adjusting and closing work formally into the ledger. These entries are shown in Figure 52, as they appear in the journal, and these entries are shown in Figure 53, as they appear when they are posted in the ledger.

The Thoron ledger, Figure 53, is shown as it appears after closing entries are made and the ledger is ruled as of December 31. Note that the Accrued and Deferred Income and the Accrued and Deferred Expense accounts have not been ruled and totaled because the bookkeeper expects to make entries in these accounts as of the opening of the next period, January 1, to retransfer these items to the income and expense accounts in which they originated. After these retransfer entries are made, the deferred and accrued accounts are totaled and ruled. 

The careful bookkeeper usually takes a trial balance of the ledger in its "December 31 midnight" condition to prove that it is in balance. Such a trial balance is known as a post-closing trial balance. Since all income and expense accounts are closed at this time, the post-closing trial balance contains only the accounts for the statement of condition.

7 The post-closing trial balance is virtually identical in content to the columns for the statement of condition on the work sheet. 

Since it is taken only to prove that the ledger is in balance, there is really no need to copy the titles and the amounts of the accounts in the usual trial balance form (except possibly as a textbook problem). Customarily, the post-closing trial balance is taken in the form of two adding machine tapes; on one tape all the debit balances in the ledger at that time are listed and added and on the other tape all the credit balances.

Thoron's post-closing trial balance, December 31, 1955, appears as follows: 

2,400 16,500 36,500 2,550 9,000 8,200 14,160 980 60 90,350* 2,590 7,350 9,750 4,640 355 5,600 300 59,765 90,350*

The Work Sheet and Interim Statements 

In most businesses management needs income statements and, to a lesser degree, statements of condition more frequently than once a year. Each month the accountant could, of course, complete the full cycle of formally adjusting and closing the books to produce monthly statements. In practice it is seldom that the books are closed fully each month since the statements can be adequately drawn up from a work sheet. This use of the work sheet-to obtain monthly income statements and statements of condition without formally closing the ledger-is probably more important than is its use to facilitate the formal closing, as illustrated in Figure 49. 

The task of preparing interim statements in this manner involves no new theory whatsoever; it requires a careful adaptation of the normal steps in bookkeeping procedure. The journals that are used in the business must be fully posted both as to detail and totals at the end of the month. A trial balance is then taken on work sheet paper. The processes of making adjusting entries and of sorting the adjusted amounts to the columns for the income statement and for the statement of condition proceed as before, even though there is no intention of using the work sheet as a guide for the making of formal entries in the ledger. The task of gathering the information necessary for the adjustments, of taking the inventories (fuel, supplies, and merchandise), of reading the meters, of inspecting the insurance policies, of accruing the interest and rent incomes, are just as much problems in the preparation of interim statements as in the preparation of annual statements at the time of the formal closing. However, there is a substantial saving in time and effort because journal entries are not made for the adjusting and closing processes.The ledger is not posted or ruled, nor are the account balances brought down in it. The statements drawn from an interim work sheet look no different from those drawn at formal closing time. 

The foregoing remarks obviously apply nicely to January, the first month of the new year, or to the first month of any fiscal year. 

But what of the income statement for February? If the expense and income accounts are not closed at the end of January, the February transactions must be superimposed on the January totals, and by the end of February, the accounts must show balances for

the two-month period. When we reflect that the amount in these accounts at the end of January is the unadjusted figure (the adjustments having been made only on the work sheet), it becomes apparent that there is no feasible way of developing the significant figures for February directly from the accounts. 

The solution to this difficulty is to prepare a work sheet for the two-month (January and February) period. The work sheet is prepared with full adjustments as of the end of the two-month period; the work sheet adjustments made at the end of the first month are disregarded. This solution results in an income statement that is correct for each item of income and expense for the two-month period. Since we have available the income statement prepared for the first month, we can produce the income statement for the second month by the direct subtraction of corresponding items.

To obtain the income statement for March, we prepare a threemonth statement and subtract from it the two-month statement and so forth throughout the year. Although this procedure involves substantial effort, it involves considerably less effort than would be required for the formal closing of the books each month. 

It should be noted that the preparation of the monthly statement of condition does not require any special effort, since the statement of condition produced at the end of each cumulative period is the same as the statement of condition produced at the end of the final month for that period.

It should be realized that the cumulative income statements, which the work sheet produces as a by-product in developing the monthly income statements, are in themselves very useful to management. In many lines of business the statement for any one month may be subject to the incidence of random incomes or costs or both which cannot be fully reflected by even the most careful accrual system. In these cases the single month's statements may be less than fully reliable for management purposes. If the period covered by the statement reflects two months' or even three months' transactions the effect of random variations is decreased and the true trend is identified with more certainty. In these cases the management may wisely pay more attention to the year-to-date figures than to those for any individual month. 

Frequently the monthly and year-to-date figures may be used

most significantly by comparing them with the corresponding figures for prior years. Although the figures from any one statement may not have significance for management, a comparison of those figures with the corresponding figures for earlier periods often reveals trends upon which management may base either specific action or even long range policy. 

It is perhaps desirable to repeat at this point what is implicit in all our work in this text: It is only when management decisions result from the statements that accounting becomes worth while. 

No matter how neat, how complicated, how interesting, or how correct accounting statements are, it is only when they result in action that they are worth more than the paper they are written on. 

The student or the accountant who keeps this principle in mind has advanced a long way toward real maturity in the profession. 

The Monthly Statement Technique Illustrated 

The technique of deriving the monthly income statement from two cumulative statements for successive months is shown in Figure 55. The cost of goods sold is set up in a separate section to avoid the confusion that "skip-jump" adding and subtracting would cause if it were performed in the single column devoted to each statement. Most of the single month's figures are derived by subtracting the figures for the three-month period from the figures for the four-month period. The merchandise inventories are not derived in quite the same way, however, since the figure for the beginning inventory is the same on each of the cumulative statements and the figure for the ending inventory is the same on the statement for the four-month period and on the statement for the single month. A little pondering reveals the reason for these facts, although at first they may seem a trifle mystifying. 

The method of combining the figures, shown in Figure 55, demonstrates the principles involved, but it is by no means the only method that can be used. It would be possible, for instance, to incorporate four extra columns in the work sheet itself to show the figures for the previous cumulative income statement and the figures derived for the latest monthly statement. These columns would be placed on the work sheet to the right of the columns for the income statement and ahead of the columns for the statement of condition. This technique widens the work sheet to

7 twelve columns but relieves us of the task of developing a separate comparative form such as that shown in Figure 55. 

This special use of the work sheet is demonstrated in Problem 4 at the end of this chapter. Columns 1 though 6 and columns 11 and 12 comprise an eight-column work sheet for the year-to-date, which in the illustration is for the eleven-month period January through November. Columns 7 and 8 copy the cumulative income statement for the previous month, and columns 9 and 10 are the income statement for the month derived by a comparison of the two cumulative statements.

The Use of Estimated Merchandise Inventories 

The taking of an actual physical inventory of merchandise each month is a burdensome task. It must be acknowledged that without the taking of a physical inventory there is no such thing as a really accurate income statement. However in some merchandising businesses, the relationship between purchase prices and selling prices is so stable that the cost of goods sold for a month can be estimated quite reliably from the sales figure which is shown on the books. For instance, if the cost of goods sold has been seventy per cent of the net sales for many months and if pricing policies have not changed, the merchant may consider that taking a physical inventory at the end of each month is a waste of time. The accountant can draw up a work sheet showing all other elements of expense and income on an accurate accrual basis but showing the cost of goods sold on a calculated basis. The income statement drawn from such a work sheet should acknowledge the estimated nature of the gross margin. The estimation of the ending inventory depends on the same basic relationship as does the estimation of the Cost of Goods Sold. 

We know the relationship: Beginning Inventory + Purchases Ending Inventory Cost of Goods Sold. Since the books contain the figures for the Beginning Inventory and the Purchases and since the Cost of Goods Sold can be calculated by applying our known percentage to the Sales figure in the ledger account, we can calculate the remaining factor of ending inventory. We do not actually need the amount of the ending inventory for the income statement, but we do need it for the statement of condition. 

All such calculations should be made on the basis of net sales

and net purchase figures. If the original calculation of the percentage were based on experience with gross sales figures and then applied to net sales figures (or the reverse) the results would of course be inaccurate. It is therefore a wise precaution to use net sales figures exclusively. 

In a particular business, it may be quite reasonable to take a physical inventory only every third month and to rely upon a calculated inventory and cost of goods sold for the two intervening months. In very special circumstances, it might be proper to run eleven monthly statements on the basis of an estimated inventory and to take a physical inventory only at the end of the year.

It should be emphasized, however, that an estimated inventory figure can be used as a basic figure only if the price relationships are known to be very stable. In a merchandising business, one important purpose of the monthly income statement is to permit management to watch the relationship between the cost of goods sold and the gross margin to see if the proper relationship is being maintained. Obviously if this relationship is based on an assumed percentage, it would be of no use to management as a check on the gross margin.

The technique of using an estimated inventory has another shortcoming. An estimated inventory fails to reveal employees' dishonesty consisting of the theft of merchandise or the theft of cash from cash sales, that is, the "knocking down of cash sales."

When dishonesty of this kind starts, it is likely to spread rapidly. 

If the monthly income statements were on an estimated inventory basis, such dishonesty might reach extremely serious proportions before being discovered. On the other hand, these forms of dishonesty show up immediately in a lowered percentage of gross margin when the cost of sales is based on the taking of a physical inventory. A sudden drop in the percentage of the gross margin to sales, when there has been no change in pricing policy, is often the first warning of dishonesty developing among his employees that a proprietor receives. Once the proprietor has reason to suspect dishonesty, usually he can trap the offender himself, by one method or another, or he can hire a "shopping service" to do it for him. 

Although employee fraud should not be overemphasized, the sad fact is that it is altogether too prevalent. It is often a factor

1 contributing to business failure, and therefore, it is of very real concern to the banker who must deal with hundreds of customers who may not know how to recognize such trouble when it occurs. 

Other Uses of the Work Sheet 

By and large the income tax return of a business is simply its income statement rewritten in the official form of the tax return. 

However, the tax laws are so intricate that there are almost always a number of differences in detail between the income statement as such and the tax return. Certain kinds of business income are not taxable. Certain business expenses either are not deductible or may be deductible only up to certain amounts or within certain limits. 

To make the transition from income as shown in the income statement to income as reported in the tax return, the accountant often uses extra columns on the work sheet; he heads these columns "Federal Tax Return." Most of the items from the income statement can be copied directly on the columns of the tax return, but if the provisions of the law require special treatment for a particular item, the legally proper figure is entered on the tax return. 

The particular reasons for these differences should be explained either in a space at the right or in footnotes. 

In some instances state income tax laws may require returns which do not agree exactly with either the Federal tax return or with the company's own income statement. This situation could be met by using still another pair of columns headed "State Tax Return." Such a work sheet would not only be an effective device for assembling originally the figures for the tax returns, but it would also be a very effective reference when questions are raised later by tax auditors or others regarding the nature of the differences
